实现零剂量儿童目标的进展:低收入和中低收入国家人口可改变的风险因素和不平等趋势分析

Progress in reaching zero-dose children: analysis of population-modifiable risk factors and inequity trends in low- and lower-middle-income countries
作者： Firew Tekle Bobo, Abdi Gari Negasa, Hawi Tekle Bobo, Kiddus Yitbarek, Girmaye Dinsa, Mirkuzie Woldie
摘要中文翻译零剂量 (ZD) 儿童仍然集中在低收入和中低收入国家 (LLMIC),是 2030 年免疫议程 (IA2030) 的一个主要焦点,该议程的目标是到 2030 年将零剂量 (ZD) 儿童数量减少一半。尽管做出了这一承诺,但在许多高负担环境中,进展仍然停滞不前。本研究旨在调查过去十年中低收入中等收入国家在帮助 ZD 儿童方面取得的进展、与财富相关的不平等趋势以及人口可改变的关键风险因素。我们分析了来自 10 个 LLMIC 的两轮最新人口与健康调查 (DHS) 数据集,以评估减少 ZD 儿童数量的进展。根据 2020 年后各轮调查的可用性,纳入国家/地区。ZD儿童被定义为12-23个月大的儿童,未接受过任何剂量的卡介苗、含百白破、脊髓灰质炎或含麻疹的疫苗。集中指数用于评估与财富相关的不平等。使用多级逻辑回归模型来识别与 ZD 状态相关的可改变的风险因素。使用比值比和 患病率 估计值计算人群归因分数 (PAF),以量化可修改风险因素的贡献。ZD 患病率 在 10 个国家中有 5 个显着增加——莫桑比克(4.8% 升至 14.2%)、科特迪瓦(4.9% 升至 8.7%)、尼泊尔(0.8% 升至 4.5%)、坦桑尼亚(2.3% 升至 4.4%)和马达加斯加(13.3% 升至 18.1%),而在剩下五个。大多数国家都存在与财富相关的不平等现象,ZD 儿童不成比例地集中在最贫困的家庭。在家分娩的 ZD 的 PAF 最高(17.1%;95% CI:14.1-19.6),其次是少于 4 次 ANC 接触(8.6%;95% CI:7.2-10.0)和不使用避孕药具(6.9%;95% CI:5.3-8.5)。生殖和孕产妇健康服务的综合贡献为(PAF = 29.4%;95% CI:24.5–33.7)。社会经济因素包括母亲缺乏教育(4.4%;95% CI:2.6-6.1)和不听广播(2.7%;95% CI:0.4-4.6)。低收入和中低收入国家在帮助 ZD 儿童方面取得的进展有所下降。需要做出新的承诺并继续努力,通过改善获得医疗服务的机会和解决家庭的社会经济挑战来惠及这些地区的 ZD 儿童。
查看研究七要素
问题与背景
该研究解决了低收入和中低收入国家 (LLMIC) 零剂量 (ZD) 儿童面临的持续挑战,尽管全球做出了承诺,但这些国家实现《2030 年免疫议程》到 2030 年将零剂量 (ZD) 人数减半的目标的进展却陷入停滞。
设计与对象
该研究分析了来自 10 个 LLMIC 的两轮最新人口与健康调查 (DHS) 数据集,以评估减少 ZD 儿童数量的进展,重点关注进行 2020 年后几轮调查的国家。
核心方法
使用多级逻辑回归模型来识别与 ZD 状态相关的可修改风险因素,并使用比值比和 患病率 估计来计算人群归因分数 (PAF),以量化这些因素的贡献。
主要结果
ZD 患病率 在 10 个国家中有 5 个显着增加——莫桑比克(4.8% 升至 14.2%)、科特迪瓦(4.9% 升至 8.7%)、尼泊尔(0.8% 升至 4.5%)、坦桑尼亚(2.3% 升至 4.4%)和马达加斯加(13.3% 升至 18.1%),而在剩下五个。
解释与创新
作者将这些发现解释为低收入和中等收入国家在帮助 ZD 儿童方面进展缓慢的证据,强调与财富相关的不平等和最贫困家庭的不成比例的负担是所观察到的趋势的关键驱动因素。
科研与公卫意义
研究结果强调,需要重新做出承诺并采取有针对性的干预措施,以改善获得卫生服务的机会并解决社会经济障碍,特别是针对最贫困家庭,以加快减少 ZD 儿童的进展。
局限与证据强度
该研究因其对 DHS 数据集的依赖而受到限制,该数据集可能无法捕获影响 ZD 状态的所有背景因素,并且横截面设计限制了因果推断;由于这些限制,证据强度被评估为中等。

Progress in reaching zero-dose children: analysis of population-modifiable risk factors and inequity trends in low- and lower-middle-income countries

Authors: Firew Tekle Bobo, Abdi Gari Negasa, Hawi Tekle Bobo, Kiddus Yitbarek, Girmaye Dinsa, Mirkuzie Woldie
Original abstract (English)
Zero-dose (ZD) children remain concentrated in low- and lower-middle-income countries (LLMICs) and are a major focus of the Immunization Agenda 2030 (IA2030), which aims to halve their number by 2030. Despite this commitment, progress has stalled in many high-burden settings. This study aimed to examine progress in reaching ZD children, trends in wealth-related inequity, and key population-modifiable risk factors across LLMICs over the past decade. We analysed two rounds of the most recent Demographic and health survey (DHS) datasets from ten LLMICs to assess progress in reducing the number of ZD children. Countries were included based on the availability of post-2020 survey rounds. ZD children were defined as children aged 12–23 months who had not received any dose of BCG, DTP-containing, polio, or measles-containing vaccines. The concentration index was used to assess wealth-related inequities. Multilevel logistic regression models were used to identify modifiable risk factors associated with ZD status. The population attributable fraction (PAF) was calculated using odds ratios and prevalence estimates to quantify the contribution of modifiable risk factors. ZD prevalence increased significantly in five of ten countries – Mozambique (4.8% to 14.2%), Côte d’Ivoire (4.9% to 8.7%), Nepal (0.8% to 4.5%), Tanzania (2.3% to 4.4%), and Madagascar (13.3% to 18.1%) — while no statistically significant change was observed in the remaining five. Wealth-related inequities were significant in most countries, with ZD children disproportionately concentrated in the poorest households. Home delivery had the highest PAF for ZD (17.1%; 95% CI: 14.1–19.6), followed by fewer than four ANC contacts (8.6%; 95% CI: 7.2–10.0) and non-use of contraceptives (6.9%; 95% CI: 5.3–8.5). The combined contribution of reproductive and maternal health services was (PAF = 29.4%; 95% CI: 24.5–33.7). Socioeconomic factors included lack of maternal education (4.4%; 95% CI: 2.6–6.1) and not listening to the radio (2.7%; 95% CI: 0.4–4.6). Progress in reaching ZD children in low- and lower-middle-income countries has declined. A renewed commitment and continued effort are needed to reach ZD children in these regions by improving access to health services and addressing the socioeconomic challenges of families.
View research seven-element analysis
Question and background
The study addressed the persistent challenge of zero-dose (ZD) children in low- and lower-middle-income countries (LLMICs), where progress toward the Immunization Agenda 2030 target to halve ZD numbers by 2030 has stalled despite global commitments.
Design and population
The study analysed two rounds of the most recent Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) datasets from ten LLMICs to assess progress in reducing the number of ZD children, focusing on countries with post-2020 survey rounds.
Core methods
Multilevel logistic regression models were used to identify modifiable risk factors associated with ZD status, and the population attributable fraction (PAF) was calculated using odds ratios and prevalence estimates to quantify the contribution of these factors.
Main results
ZD prevalence increased significantly in five of ten countries—Mozambique (4.8% to 14.2%), Côte d’Ivoire (4.9% to 8.7%), Nepal (0.8% to 4.5%), Tanzania (2.3% to 4.4%), and Madagascar (13.3% to 18.1%)—while no statistically significant change was observed in the remaining five.
Interpretation and novelty
The authors interpreted the findings as evidence of declining progress in reaching ZD children in LLMICs, highlighting wealth-related inequities and the disproportionate burden among the poorest households as key drivers of the observed trends.
Scientific and public-health significance
The findings underscore the need for renewed commitment and targeted interventions to improve access to health services and address socioeconomic barriers, particularly for the poorest households, to accelerate progress toward reducing ZD children.
Limitations and evidence strength
The study is limited by its reliance on DHS datasets, which may not capture all contextual factors influencing ZD status, and the cross-sectional design restricts causal inference; the evidence strength is assessed as moderate due to these constraints.

A 高优先级学术文献 · 综述A high priorityAcademic literature · Review

2025 年新墨西哥州 麻疹 暴发 的经济影响:强调预防未来 麻疹 的努力成本

Economic Impact of the 2025 New Mexico Measles Outbreak: Highlighting the Cost of Efforts to Prevent Future Outbreaks
作者： Jamison Pike, Nora Holzinger, Han (Tiffany) Xiao, Emma Stanislawski, Jessica Leung, Andrea Romero, Chad Smelser
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本次审查重点关注 麻疹病毒,考察 2025 年新墨西哥州 麻疹 暴发 的经济影响。核心问题是估计 暴发 的社会经济负担,并强调支持 暴发 控制和预防未来 暴发 的 疫苗接种 相关活动的公共卫生支出。
证据基础与方法
该审查从社会角度进行了成本分析,估算了三类成本:公共卫生应对成本、直接医疗成本和生产力损失。该研究包括 2025 年 2 月至 9 月期间新墨西哥州的 9 个县,收集了 133 名公共卫生响应人员、100 名确诊为 麻疹 的患者以及 205 名接触者的数据。该方法将成本分为响应成本(劳动力、材料、旅行)、直接医疗成本(第三方)或生产力损失(疾病、隔离、检疫、护理)。
共识与结论
The overall societal cost of the outbreak was estimated at $5.4 million, with $53,522 per case and $1,817 per contact. The largest component of the public health response was vaccination-related activities, which accounted for about $2.1 million of the $3.2 million total response costs. The study concludes that these estimates provide important insights for decision-making by policymakers and public health stakeholders regarding budgets for and expansion of vaccination coverage during an outbreak and strengthening outbreak preparedness.
争议与缺口
审查承认,由于报告不完整,成本可能被高估或低估。使用保险数据代替实际医疗费用,并且排除某些直接医疗费用。对生产力损失进行了假设,这给研究结果带来了不确定性。此外,该研究并未解决犬瘟热病毒或风疹病毒等相关病毒的潜在影响,这些病毒在操作或生物学上相关,但不是主要焦点。
科研与实践启示
该研究强调了通过 疫苗 的高覆盖率来保护社区免受 麻疹 等 麻疹 等可预防疾病的社会价值。它还强调需要改进数据收集和报告,以更好地估计 暴发 的经济负担。此外,它还表明了投资公共卫生准备和 疫苗接种 计划的重要性,以防止未来出现 暴发 并降低相关成本。

Economic Impact of the 2025 New Mexico Measles Outbreak: Highlighting the Cost of Efforts to Prevent Future Outbreaks

Authors: Jamison Pike, Nora Holzinger, Han (Tiffany) Xiao, Emma Stanislawski, Jessica Leung, Andrea Romero, Chad Smelser
Original abstract (English)
BACKGROUND: In 2025, the United States experienced its highest measles case count since 1992. In New Mexico, 100 confirmed measles cases (99 outbreak-related cases), including 1 death, occurred across 9 counties from February through September 2025. OBJECTIVE: To estimate the societal economic burden of this outbreak while highlighting public health expenditures for vaccination-related activities that supported outbreak control and prevention of future outbreaks. DESIGN: Cost analysis from a societal perspective to estimate 3 types of costs: public health response costs, direct medical costs, and productivity losses. SETTING: Nine counties in New Mexico during February to September 2025. PARTICIPANTS: 133 public health responders, 100 patients with confirmed measles, and 205 exposed contacts. INTERVENTION: Public health response activities, including vaccination campaigns, case management, and contact tracing. MEASUREMENTS: Costs were categorized as response costs (labor, materials, travel), direct medical costs (third-party), or productivity losses (illness, isolation, quarantine, caregiving). RESULTS: The overall societal cost was estimated at $5.4 million ($53 522 per case, or $1817 per contact). The largest component of the public health response to the outbreak (about $3.2 million) was costs from vaccination-related activities (about $2.1 million). LIMITATIONS: Costs may be overestimated or underestimated due to incomplete reporting; insurance data were used rather than actual medical costs; certain direct medical costs were excluded; and assumptions were made for productivity losses. CONCLUSION: These estimates offer important insights for decision making by policymakers and public health stakeholders regarding budgets for and expansion of vaccination coverage during an outbreak and strengthening outbreak preparedness. This study also highlights the societal value of protecting communities from vaccine-preventable diseases like measles, which can be effectively prevented through high vaccination coverage. PRIMARY FUNDING SOURCE: None.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on the measles virus, examining the economic impact of the 2025 New Mexico measles outbreak. The central question is to estimate the societal economic burden of the outbreak and highlight public health expenditures for vaccination-related activities that supported outbreak control and prevention of future outbreaks.
Evidence base and method
The review conducted a cost analysis from a societal perspective, estimating three types of costs: public health response costs, direct medical costs, and productivity losses. The study included nine counties in New Mexico during February to September 2025, with data collected on 133 public health responders, 100 patients with confirmed measles, and 205 exposed contacts. The methodology involved categorizing costs into response costs (labor, materials, travel), direct medical costs (third-party), or productivity losses (illness, isolation, quarantine, caregiving).
Consensus and conclusions
The overall societal cost of the outbreak was estimated at $5.4 million, with $53,522 per case and $1,817 per contact. The largest component of the public health response was vaccination-related activities, which accounted for about $2.1 million of the $3.2 million total response costs. The study concludes that these estimates provide important insights for decision-making by policymakers and public health stakeholders regarding budgets for and expansion of vaccination coverage during an outbreak and strengthening outbreak preparedness.
Controversies and gaps
The review acknowledges that costs may be overestimated or underestimated due to incomplete reporting. Insurance data were used instead of actual medical costs, and certain direct medical costs were excluded. Assumptions were made for productivity losses, which introduces uncertainty into the findings. Additionally, the study does not address the potential impact of related viruses such as canine distemper virus or rubella virus, which are operationally or biologically related but not the primary focus.
Research and practice implications
The study highlights the societal value of protecting communities from vaccine-preventable diseases like measles through high vaccination coverage. It also emphasizes the need for improved data collection and reporting to better estimate the economic burden of outbreaks. Furthermore, it suggests the importance of investing in public health preparedness and vaccination programs to prevent future outbreaks and reduce associated costs.

疫苗 在蛋白质限制饮食中患有先天性代谢缺陷的儿童中的抗体反应:一个被忽视的沉默风险?

Vaccine antibody responses in children with inborn errors of metabolism on protein-restricted diets: an overlooked silent risk?
作者： Nimet Pınar Yılmazbaş, Nafiye Emel Çakar, Sevil Yıldız, Gamze Karamanlı, Melda Fatma İdrisoğlu, Sevil YILDIZ
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该研究的重点是评估患有先天性代谢缺陷(IEM)的儿童在蛋白质限制饮食中的 疫苗 抗体反应,特别是与健康儿童进行比较。核心问题是这些饮食限制是否影响 免疫应答 至 疫苗,特别是 麻疹 和乙型肝炎,并确定影响这些反应的因素。
证据基础与方法
该综述基于一项涉及 25 名氨基酸代谢紊乱(苯丙酮尿症、有机酸血症、高胱氨酸尿症和尿素循环障碍)患者和 58 名相同年龄和性别的健康儿童的研究。该研究比较了 麻疹 和乙型肝炎抗原的血清阳性率和抗体滴度,并调整了年龄和其他因素,例如每日蛋白质摄入量、饮食持续时间和血液参数。
共识与结论
研究发现,患者组乙型肝炎血清阳性率为 64%,麻疹 血清阳性率为 80%,均低于对照组。即使在调整年龄后,这些结果仍然保持一致,表明蛋白质限制饮食对 疫苗 抗体反应的潜在影响。然而,疾病亚组之间没有观察到显着差异。
争议与缺口
该研究承认,由于样本量有限,其研究结果需要在更大规模的前瞻性研究中得到证实。此外,它没有提供有关抗体反应减少的机制的详细信息,也没有提供这些发现对 IEM 患者中 疫苗 疗效的长期影响的详细信息。
科研与实践启示
该研究表明,在 IEM 儿童的代谢随访过程中应审查 疫苗 抗体反应。它强调需要进一步研究来证实这些发现并探索影响该人群中 免疫应答 的因素。

Vaccine antibody responses in children with inborn errors of metabolism on protein-restricted diets: an overlooked silent risk?

Authors: Nimet Pınar Yılmazbaş, Nafiye Emel Çakar, Sevil Yıldız, Gamze Karamanlı, Melda Fatma İdrisoğlu, Sevil YILDIZ
Original abstract (English)
OBJECTIVES: Despite routine vaccination recommendations, concerns remain about vaccine-induced immune responses in some patients with inborn errors of metabolism (IEM). Therefore, antibody levels against vaccine antigens should be monitored to detect possible immune dysfunction. The aim of this study is to evaluate the vaccine antibody responses among children with IEM who have to follow protein restricted diet, and to compare with those in healthy children, and also to identify the factors affecting vaccine antibody responses in these patients. METHODS: Anti-HBs and measles immunoglobulin G results of 25 patients diagnosed with amino acid metabolism disorder; phenylketonuria, organic acidemia, homocystinuria and urea cycle disorder, and 58 healthy children of the same age and gender were recorded. Seropositivity rates and titers were compared between two groups, and also with age, daily protein intake, diet duration, total protein, albumin and hemoglobin levels. RESULTS: Seropositivity against hepatitis B was 64 %, and against measles was 80 % in the patient group, and these seropositivity rates were lower than those in the control group. These results show that the trend of the findings was maintained even after adjustment for age. No significant differences in vaccine antibody responses was found among disease subgroups. CONCLUSIONS: As children receiving long-term protein-restricted diets may demonstrate reduced vaccine antibody responses, these findings warrant confirmation in larger prospective studies. We believe that vaccine antibody responses should also be reviewed during metabolic follow-up in these patients.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The study focuses on evaluating vaccine antibody responses in children with inborn errors of metabolism (IEM) on protein-restricted diets, specifically comparing them to healthy children. The central question is whether these dietary restrictions affect immune responses to vaccines, particularly measles and hepatitis B, and identifying factors influencing these responses.
Evidence base and method
The review is based on a single study involving 25 patients with amino acid metabolism disorders (phenylketonuria, organic acidemia, homocystinuria, and urea cycle disorder) and 58 healthy children of the same age and gender. The study compared seropositivity rates and titers of antibodies against measles and hepatitis B antigens, adjusting for age and other factors such as daily protein intake, diet duration, and blood parameters.
Consensus and conclusions
The study found that seropositivity against hepatitis B was 64% and against measles was 80% in the patient group, both lower than in the control group. These results remained consistent even after adjusting for age, indicating a potential impact of protein-restricted diets on vaccine antibody responses. However, no significant differences were observed among disease subgroups.
Controversies and gaps
The study acknowledges that its findings need confirmation in larger prospective studies due to the limited sample size. Additionally, it does not provide detailed information on the mechanisms underlying the reduced antibody responses or the long-term implications of these findings for vaccine efficacy in IEM patients.
Research and practice implications
The study suggests that vaccine antibody responses should be reviewed during metabolic follow-up in children with IEM. It highlights the need for further research to confirm these findings and to explore the factors affecting immune responses in this population.

膀胱癌 治疗 的最新溶瘤病毒:系统评价

Updated oncolytic viruses for the treatment of bladder cancer: a systematic review
作者： Fatemeh Khatami, Abdolreza Mohammadi, Kayhan Azadmanesh, Roham Deyhimfar, Helia Azodian Ghajar, Parsa Nikoofar, Seyed Mohammad Kazem Aghamir
摘要中文翻译背景和目的:膀胱癌(BCa)是最常见的泌尿道恶性肿瘤,通常在晚期才被诊断出来。复发仍然是当前 治疗 策略的主要挑战。在过去的十年中,溶瘤病毒 (OV) 的抗肿瘤潜力日益得到认可,多项研究评估了它们在 BCa 治疗 中的安全性和有效性。在这篇系统综述中,我们提出了临床试验和动物研究,以全面评估各种 OV 在 BCa 中的安全性和治疗效率。材料和方法:收到 PROSPERO 注册码后,系统地搜索了四个电子数据库 PubMed、Web of Science、Scopus 和 Embase,以识别评估 OV 在 BCa 中的安全性和 治疗 潜力的研究。两名评审员独立进行筛选过程,并将临床试验和动物研究的数据系统地提取到Excel中以供后续合成和报告。本次系统评价的所有步骤均遵循《Cochrane 干预系统评价手册》中概述的指南,坚持方法严谨性和透明度的高标准。结果:初始搜索策略产生 1,061 条记录。删除重复项后,对 809 篇文章的相关性进行了筛选。排除 761 项研究后,有 48 项研究被认为符合数据提取条件,其中包括 17 项临床试验和 29 项动物研究。研究了各种溶瘤病毒,无论是作为单一疗法还是与其他药物联合使用,包括牛痘病毒、腺病毒、溶瘤腺病毒 ONYX、柯萨奇病毒 A21 (CVA21)、溶瘤 CVA21 (V937)、减毒 麻疹病毒(MV-NIS 病毒)、重组腺病毒 (rAd) 和经工程改造的血清型 5 腺病毒表达GM-CSF。所有研究都报告了在安全参数范围内最有效的剂量。结论:常见病毒为非复制重组腺病毒血清型5(Ad5),其编码人干扰素α-2b(IFNα2b),一种具有抗肿瘤特性的细胞因子。它通过膀胱内滴注进行给药,并已获得 FDA 批准

科研与实践启示
复发仍然是当前 治疗 策略的主要挑战。

Updated oncolytic viruses for the treatment of bladder cancer: a systematic review

Authors: Fatemeh Khatami, Abdolreza Mohammadi, Kayhan Azadmanesh, Roham Deyhimfar, Helia Azodian Ghajar, Parsa Nikoofar, Seyed Mohammad Kazem Aghamir
Original abstract (English)
Background and Objectives: Bladder cancer (BCa) is the most prevalent malignancy of the urinary tract and is often diagnosed at a late stage. Recurrence remains a major challenge for current treatment strategies. Over the past decade, the antitumor potential of oncolytic viruses (OVs) has been increasingly recognized, with several studies evaluating their safety and efficacy in BCa treatment. In this systematic review, we present clinical trials and animal studies to comprehensively evaluate the safety and therapeutic efficiency of various OVs in BCa. Materials and Methods: After receiving the PROSPERO registration code, four electronic databases PubMed, Web of Sci- ence, Scopus, and Embase, were systematically searched to identify studies evaluating the safety and treatment potential of OVs in BCa. Two reviewers independently conducted the screening process, and data from clinical trials and animal studies were systematically extracted into Excel for subsequent synthesis and reporting. All steps of this systematic review adhered to the guidelines outlined in the Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews of Interventions, upholding high standards of methodological rigor and transparency. Results: The initial search strategy yielded 1,061 records. After removing duplicates, 809 articles were screened for rel- evance. Following the exclusion of 761 studies, 48 were deemed eligible for data extraction, comprising 17 clinical trials and 29 animal studies. Various oncolytic viruses were investigated, either as monotherapies or in combination with other agents, including Vaccinia virus, Adenovirus, Oncolytic adenovirus ONYX, Coxsackievirus A21 (CVA21), oncolytic CVA21 (V937), attenuated measles virus (MV-NIS virus), recombinant adenovirus (rAd), and a serotype 5 adenovirus engineered to express GM-CSF. All studies reported their most effective doses administered within safe parameters. Conclusion: The common virus was the non-replicative recombinant adenovirus serotype 5 (Ad5), which encodes human interferon alfa-2b (IFNα2b) a cytokine with anti-tumor properties. It is administered via intravesical instillation and has received FDA approval

Evidence base and method
Materials and Methods: After receiving the PROSPERO registration code, four electronic databases PubMed, Web of Sci- ence, Scopus, and Embase, were systematically searched to identify studies evaluating the safety and treatment potential of OVs in BCa.
Consensus and conclusions

Controversies and gaps
All steps of this systematic review adhered to the guidelines outlined in the Cochrane Handbook for Systematic Reviews of Interventions, upholding high standards of methodological rigor and transparency.
Research and practice implications
Recurrence remains a major challenge for current treatment strategies.

基因解析的同义密码子排序揭示了鲸类麻疹病毒中保守的 CpG 回避

Gene-resolved synonymous codon ordering reveals conserved CpG avoidance in cetacean morbillivirus

除了免疫缺陷之外:索马里的 麻疹 复苏、卫生系统脆弱性和儿童 死亡率 风险

Beyond immunity gaps: measles resurgence, health-system fragility, and child mortality risk in Somalia
作者： Abdullahi Hassan Elmi, Fahmo Hussein Ibrahim

Beyond immunity gaps: measles resurgence, health-system fragility, and child mortality risk in Somalia

Authors: Abdullahi Hassan Elmi, Fahmo Hussein Ibrahim

2026 年美国 麻疹 病例创 35 年来新高 - Healthline

U.S. Measles Cases Hit Record-Breaking 35-Year High in 2026 - Healthline
新闻简报根据约翰·霍普金斯大学美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 的数据,2026 年,美国 麻疹 病例创下 35 年来的新高,截至 7 月 31 日确诊病例为 2,371 例。这一激增归因于 麻疹 的犹豫和免疫率下降,以及 麻疹 在全球的高流通量。大多数病例(94%)与 37 个新的 暴发 有关,其中南卡罗来纳州和犹他州是值得注意的热点地区。CDC 报告称,93% 的病例涉及未接种疫苗的个体或 疫苗接种 状态未知的个体,2025 例病例中 90% 与 48 个 疫苗接种 相关。由于泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 将于 11 月对数据进行评估,美国面临失去自 2000 年以来一直享有的 麻疹 豁免地位的风险。联邦卫生官员淡化了 麻疹,但 CDC 数据显示,2026 年国际游客中仅报告了 16 例病例。这种情况凸显了由于公共卫生问题和 疫苗 信心问题,维持 麻疹 控制所面临的持续挑战。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于2026年8月4日。截至2026年7月31日,2026年麻疹案件达到35年来新高。
地点与对象
美国,特别是包括南卡罗来纳州和犹他州等 45 个司法管辖区,麻疹 案件大幅增加。受影响人群包括未接种疫苗的人和 疫苗接种 状态未知的人,93% 的病例归因于他们。
事件
2026年美国麻疹病例数超过2025年总数,创35年来新高。这是由于 疫苗接种 的犹豫和 疫苗接种 汇率的下降,以及 麻疹 全球的高流通量。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年7月31日,美国已有2371例麻疹确诊病例,新增37例麻疹,占确诊病例的94%。2025年,共有2,289例病例,导致3人死亡、243人住院。CDC 报告称,2025 例病例中 90% 与 48 个 暴发 有关。
应对、状态与不确定性
面临失去自 2000 年以来一直存在的 麻疹 豁免地位的风险。泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 计划于 11 月评估美国 麻疹 数据。联邦卫生官员淡化了 麻疹,但 CDC 数据显示,2026 年国际游客中仅报告了 16 例 麻疹 病例。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

U.S. Measles Cases Hit Record-Breaking 35-Year High in 2026 - Healthline

News Brief
In 2026, the United States experienced a record-breaking 35-year high in measles cases, with 2,371 confirmed cases as of July 31, according to the Johns Hopkins U.S. Center for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). This surge is attributed to vaccination hesitancy and declining immunization rates, as well as high measles circulation globally. The majority of cases, 94%, were linked to 37 new outbreaks, with South Carolina and Utah being notable hotspots. The CDC reports that 93% of cases involved unvaccinated individuals or those with unknown vaccination status, and 90% of 2025 cases were associated with 48 outbreaks. The U.S. is at risk of losing its measles exemption status, which it has held since 2000, as the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) is set to evaluate the data in November. Federal health officials have downplayed the outbreaks, but CDC data indicate that only 16 cases were reported among international visitors in 2026. The situation highlights the ongoing challenges in maintaining measles control due to public health concerns and vaccine confidence issues.

Subscribe Sign in Health NewsFact Checked U.S. May Lose Measles Exemption Status As 2026 Cases Hit a 35-Year High Written by David Mills — Fact checked by Jill Seladi-Schulman, Ph.D. — Updated on August 4, 2026 Add Healthline on Google Share on Pinterest The number of measles cases in the United States in 2026 has surpassed the total for 2025, marking a 35-year high. Jan Sonnenmair/Getty Images The number of measles cases in the United States in 2026 surpassed the total for 2025, marking the highest number in 35 years. The United States is at risk of losing its measles elimination status, which it has held since 2000. The United Kingdom, several European countries, and Canada recently lost their measles-free statuses. Experts attribute the upward trend to vaccination hesitancy and conflicting messaging from federal health officials. The United States is poised to lose its measles exemption status, which it has held for the past quarter-century. The total number of measles cases in the United States has hit a 35-year high, reaching 2,371 cases as of July 31, according to the Johns Hopkins U.S. Measles Tracker. This figure exceeds the total number of measles cases in the United States in 2025. In 2025, the United States recorded a 33-year high in measles cases, with the CDC confirming 2,289 casesTrusted Source nationwide. That was the highest level of U.S. measles cases since 1992Trusted Source. The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), an arm of the World Health Organization (WHO), is scheduled to evaluate U.S. measles data in November. Last year, PAHO officials announced that Canada had lost its measles exemption status due to ongoing outbreaks. Earlier this year, the World Health Organization (WHO) announced the United Kingdom and five European countries had lost their measles-free status due to rising infection rates across the continent. Many experts believe that the United States is next. The U.S. measles exemption status has been in place since 2000, when only 86 casesTrusted Source were reported to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). As the number of cases in 2026 surpasses last year’s total, vaccine hesitancy and declining vaccination rates are likely factors. “Measles is a highly contagious disease that can turn deadly,” said Anne Liu, MD, a clinical associate professor of pediatrics, immunology, and allergy at Stanford University. “People who are unvaccinated and people who are immunocompromised should be quite concerned about the growing footprint of this outbreak,” Liu told Healthline. U.S. measles cases are still rising As of July 30, the CDC confirmedTrusted Source 2,371 measles cases in 45 U.S. jurisdictions: Alabama, Alaska, Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, District of Columbia, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Illinois, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York City, New York State, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Vermont, Virginia, Washington, Wisconsin, and Wyoming. There have been 37 new outbreaks in 2026, accounting for 94% of confirmed cases. An estimated 93% of cases are attributed to unvaccinated individuals or those with unknown vaccination status. The bulk of these cases occurred in South Carolina, which had the highest number of cases per state in U.S. history since the disease was declared eliminated. The South Carolina Department of Public Health declared the outbreak over on April 26, with 997 cases reported in Spartanburg County. Meanwhile, measles cases are climbing in other states, particularly Utah, which has become the new center of the outbreak. The state’s Department of Health and Human Services confirmed 514 measles cases in 2026. By comparison, there were 2,289 measles cases in 2025, with three deaths and 243 hospitalizations attributed to last year’s outbreaks. About a quarter of the cases in 2025 were in children under 5 years of age. Around 93% of cases occurred in unvaccinated people or those with unknown vaccination status. CDC officials reportedTrusted Source that 90% of the 2025 cases were associated with 48 outbreaks in various regions of the country. “Measles cases were high in 2025 due to declining vaccination in the U.S. and high measles circulation globally,” said Nathan Lo, MD, an assistant professor of medicine at Stanford University. Lo said that measles cases will continue their upward trend. “Measles cases continue to demonstrate sustained circulation in the U.S., which suggests the U.S. will have another year of high measles cases,” Lo said. Meanwhile, federal health officials appeared to downplay ongoing measles outbreaks. CDC principal deputy director Ralph Abraham claimed that the spread is “just the cost of doing business,” suggesting transmission was inevitable due to international travel to and from the United States. However, CDC dataTrusted Source show that, in 2026, only 16 measles cases were reported among international visitors to the United States. Medication & Treatments Symptom Management Living Well Quitting Smoking Asthma & COPD Enter your email JOIN NOW Your privacy is important to us Measles is highly contagious, but also preventable Experts say there is a simple reason why measles can spread so rapidly. It’s one of the most contagious diseasesTrusted Source on the planet. “It’s unbelievably contagious. It’s the most contagious disease we’ve ever seen,” said Danelle Fisher, MD, a pediatrician at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA. Fisher said that people with measles can be contagious 4 days before symptoms emerge and 7 to 10 days afterward. “With continued vaccine hesitancy, and the number of mistruths on social media and the community, and the confusing and conflicting recommendations coming from the FDA and CDC, there is every reason to suspect that more parents/guardians will decline routine childhood vaccinations, including measles vaccinations,” said Graham Tse, MD, a pediatrician and chief medical officer of MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, CA. “It is likely that measles outbreaks will continue, and the numbers could rise in 2026,” Tse told Healthline. Recent research has demonstrated the impacts of vaccine hesitancy. A 2025 study published in JAMA Network OpenTrusted Source found that measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) vaccination rates remained below the 93% herd immunity threshold. The study examined 149,000 children in Ohio spanning 20 months from the initial onset of the 2025 measles outbreak. William Schaffner, MD, an infectious disease specialist and professor of medicine at Vanderbilt University in Nashville, Tennessee, reaffirmed the importance of measles vaccination. “Vaccination is the way to prevent measles. There are no other ways,” Schaffner told Healthline. Other experts agreed. “The vast majority of measles cases are in unvaccinated individuals, including in children too young to be vaccinated,” Lo said. “The best way to prevent measles and its spread is through vaccination.” “Measles is a highly contagious disease so one infected person can spread it to many others,” Tse added. Changes to childhood vaccine guidelines In early January, federal health officials released new vaccination recommendations for children. The guidelines reduce the number of primarily targeted diseases from 16 to what the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) has described as the 11 “most serious infectious diseases.” However, a federal judge temporarily blocked these recommendations on March 16 after major medical groups filed a lawsuit against HHS. The Trump administration is expected to overturn the ruling. Vaccination against measles is still recommended as are inoculations against chickenpox, polioTrusted Source, and whooping cough. The CDC, however, revised recommendations on six other diseases to include only children who are at higher risk of infection. Those diseases include hepatitis and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). CDC officials added that vaccinations for children against influenza, COVID-19, and rotavirus should be based on “shared clinical decision-making,” which means parents must consult with a healthcare professional before their children receive the vaccinations. Experts say these new guidelines could led to confusion or skepticism that will further lower childhood vaccination rates. “The changes to the childhood vaccination schedule and subsequent conflicting recommendations with national organizations and states now create confusion with parents and guardians and ultimately may cause increased vaccine declination,” Tse said. Medically reviewed COPD Nutrition Guide: 5 Diet Tips Start here to learn diet tips for COPD or dive deeper into another topic. Read More 4 Steps for Managing a Flare-UpUnderstanding Your Treatment OptionsThe Best Places to Live with COPDTips for Your Home If You Have COPD What to know about measles Measles caused more than 3 million illnesses annually in the United States before the vaccine became widely available in the mid-1950s. Those cases resulted in 48,000 hospitalizations and 500 deaths annually. The virus is transmitted through the air or by direct contact with a surface that has droplets that contain the virus. Symptoms will usually appear within 14 daysTrusted Source. Those symptoms include: widespread skin rash high fever cough sore throat About 30% of measles cases lead to some form of complication, the most common being ear infections and diarrhea. Serious complications are rare, but they include: pneumonia encephalitis subacute sclerosing panencephalitis Liu said there is also a long-term consequence to measles. “One of the lesser-known features of measles is that it can wipe out part of a person’s immune memory from prior infections, leaving them vulnerable to infections they have previously had and successfully battled,” she said. How to lower your risk of measles Experts say there are precautions you can take to lower your risk of contracting measles. The number one way is through vaccination. “The best way to prevent the spread of measles is through routine vaccination for measles,” said Tse. “Once enough of the population has immunity to measles through vaccination, the measles virus cannot spread easily and cause outbreaks.” Fisher urges parents to ensure that children have received their recommended vaccines. She also recommended that adults who are unvaccinated or are unsure about their vaccination status get immunized. She said the vaccines not only protect an individual but also children and adults who can’t get vaccinated due to medical treatments or compromised immune systems. “It’s incumbent on all of us to protect the most vulnerable in our society,” Fisher said. Schaffner agreed. “There are many children and adults living among us who are immunocompromised. If they were to contract measles, they would get very sick,” he said. “We all protect them by being vaccinated and creating a ‘cocoon’ of protection around them, making it difficult for the virus to get past us to find them.” Fisher cautioned unvaccinated people from taking planes, trains, or any other form of mass transportation. She said this group should only consider essential travel. She added that you can also make adjustments at your job to lower the number of people you come into contact with. People who work at or visit schools or healthcare facilities should take extra precautions. In addition, Fisher advised everyone to be cautious around anyone with a cough or other symptoms of measles. “Be very wary of anyone with any kind of upper respiratory illness,” she said. Even going to the grocery store might be risky for those who aren’t vaccinated. Fisher added that people who have had the disease or are fully vaccinated don’t have much to worry about. “You’re not going to get sick and you’re not going to spread it,” she said. 麻疹 暴发!美国如何让一种已被击败的疾病死灰复燃——《人民药房》

Measles Outbreaks! How America Resurrected a Defeated Disease - The People's Pharmacy
新闻简报The United States has experienced a significant measles outbreak in 2026, with the CDC confirming 2,318 cases as of July 23, surpassing the total number of cases reported in all of 2025. This marks the highest number of measles cases in the country in 35 years, with 35 outbreaks and 45 jurisdictions reporting cases. Nearly half of the cases involved children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent were children under 5. While there have been no reported deaths in 2026, three deaths were recorded in 2025, including two previously healthy children. The outbreak has raised concerns about underreporting, as many cases may go untested or misdiagnosed, especially in communities with vaccine hesitancy. Additionally, measles can lead to severe complications such as pneumonia, encephalitis, and even subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare but fatal neurological condition. The CDC estimates that SSPE may develop in seven to 11 individuals per 100,000 cases, with higher risks for children infected before age 2. The long-term consequences of this outbreak could become apparent as early as the 2030s, highlighting the need for continued vigilance and vaccination efforts.

截至2026年7月23日,今年还剩五个多月(N4)。

United States, with 45 jurisdictions reporting cases and 69 percent of patients younger than 20 (N10).
事件
CDC 2026 年在美国确认了 2,318 例 麻疹 病例,创下 35 年来的最大数量(N3、N6)。
规模、影响与风险
There were 35 newly reported outbreaks, 151 hospitalizations, and 93 percent of cases linked to outbreaks. Nearly half of all cases occurred among children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent involved children under 5. No reported measles deaths in 2026, but three deaths were reported in 2025 (N10, N12, N14).
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC 估计,每 100,000 例报告的 麻疹 病例中,大约有 7 至 11 人罹患 SSPE,并且 2 岁之前感染的儿童的风险可能更高。2026 年 麻疹 的最终后果可能要到 2030 年代才能完全显现(N50、N52)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles Outbreaks! How America Resurrected a Defeated Disease - The People's Pharmacy

News Brief
The United States has experienced a significant measles outbreak in 2026, with the CDC confirming 2,318 cases as of July 23, surpassing the total number of cases reported in all of 2025. This marks the highest number of measles cases in the country in 35 years, with 35 outbreaks and 45 jurisdictions reporting cases. Nearly half of the cases involved children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent were children under 5. While there have been no reported deaths in 2026, three deaths were recorded in 2025, including two previously healthy children. The outbreak has raised concerns about underreporting, as many cases may go untested or misdiagnosed, especially in communities with vaccine hesitancy. Additionally, measles can lead to severe complications such as pneumonia, encephalitis, and even subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare but fatal neurological condition. The CDC estimates that SSPE may develop in seven to 11 individuals per 100,000 cases, with higher risks for children infected before age 2. The long-term consequences of this outbreak could become apparent as early as the 2030s, highlighting the need for continued vigilance and vaccination efforts.
Original source text (English)
The United States has broken another record. Unfortunately, this is not one anyone should celebrate. As of July 23, 2026, the CDC had confirmed 2,318 cases of measles in the United States. That is already more than the 2,289 cases reported during all of 2025, with more than five months still remaining in the year. Measles outbreaks are back with a vengeance! This is the largest number of measles cases the country has recorded in 35 years. The last time the total was higher was 1991, when 9,643 cases were reported. That was nine years before the United States declared measles eliminated. The obvious question is: How did this country resurrect a disease it had already defeated? Measles Outbreaks Set a 35-Year Record The latest CDC numbers are sobering: - 2,318 confirmed measles cases in 2026 - 35 newly reported outbreaks - 45 jurisdictions reporting cases - 151 hospitalizations - 93 percent of cases linked to outbreaks - 93 percent of patients unvaccinated or of unknown vaccination status - 69 percent of patients younger than 20 Nearly half of all cases have occurred among children and teenagers between 5 and 19 years old. Another 20 percent have involved children under 5. There have been no reported measles deaths in the United States so far this year. That is welcome news, but it should not be interpreted to mean the disease is harmless. Three Americans died from measles in 2025, including two previously healthy children. Of this year’s 456 patients under age 5, 45 have required hospitalization. Among adults with measles, approximately one in ten has been hospitalized. How Accurate if the FDA’s Measles Count? Do you trust the CDC? There have been substantial staff cuts. This is partially a political issue because anything associated with vaccination these days is political. We suspect that the measles outbreaks, as dramatic as they seem, are undercounting actual cases. Families may not seek medical attention for a child who is sick. That may be especially true in religious communities where there is some reluctance to engage with conventional medicine or public health authorities. Many patients may not be tested. Some cases may be mild or atypical. Since most physicians have never seen measles before, they might misdiagnosis it not report it. “Measles Was No Big Deal When I Was a Child” Whenever we write about measles outbreaks, readers remind us that nearly everyone used to catch the disease. C. offered this memory: “Back in the days of my youth, measles was a ‘rite of passage,’ i.e., everyone had measles, mumps and rubella. Yes, some kids were disabled to various degrees but not that many compared to all the kids who were infected. Measles was part of life.” Ron agreed: “When I and my children were kids, getting the measles was nothing major. In fact, usually, kids would get them together. It really was not a big problem.” We understand why people remember measles that way. Before the vaccine became available in 1963, almost every American child eventually caught it. Most survived without an obvious lasting disability. But that does not mean measles was benign. Before vaccination, the CDC estimates that 3 million to 4 million Americans were infected every year. Roughly 48,000 were hospitalized, 1,000 developed encephalitis and between 400 and 500 died annually. The disease seemed ordinary because it was common—not because it was safe. Measles Outbreaks Cause More Than a Rash Measles usually begins with high fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes. Tiny white spots called Koplik spots may appear inside the mouth. A blotchy red rash then spreads from the face down the body. That description can make measles sound like chickenpox with a worse cold. It is not. Measles can cause: - Pneumonia - Severe dehydration - Ear infections - Hearing loss - Encephalitis, or inflammation of the brain - Permanent neurological injury - Death Children under 5, adults over 20, pregnant women and people with weakened immune systems are especially vulnerable to severe complications. There is also a rare, delayed complication with a name few people have ever heard: subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, or SSPE. A child can seemingly recover completely from measles and remain well for years. Then, usually seven to ten years later, persistent measles virus in the brain can trigger progressive neurological deterioration. SSPE may begin with behavioral changes, poor school performance or involuntary movements. It can progress to seizures, loss of vision, dementia, coma and death. It is almost always fatal. The CDC estimates that SSPE develops in roughly seven to 11 people for every 100,000 reported measles cases. The risk may be substantially higher when children catch measles before age 2. In other words, the final consequences of the 2026 measles outbreaks may not become fully visible until the 2030s. Measles Can Erase Part of the Immune System’s Memory One of the strangest consequences of measles is something scientists call immune amnesia. The immune system normally remembers bacteria and viruses it has encountered before. That memory helps the body respond more rapidly the next time a familiar germ appears. Measles can destroy some of those memory cells. After the rash and fever disappear, a child may look completely recovered. But the immune system can remain less capable of recognizing other infectious threats for months or even years. That means the damage from measles may extend beyond the measles infection itself. A child could become more susceptible to influenza, pneumonia or other illnesses that the immune system previously knew how to fight. It is almost as though measles breaks into the immune system’s library and tears pages out of its reference books. Why Measles Outbreaks Spread Like Wildfire Measles is one of the most contagious infections known. If one person with measles enters a room containing ten susceptible people, as many as nine may become infected. The virus spreads through the air when someone breathes, coughs or sneezes. Viral particles can remain suspended for as long as two hours after the infected person has left. People can also spread measles before they realize they are sick. An infected person is contagious for approximately four days before the rash appears and four days afterward. That makes contact tracing enormously difficult. A person could fly through a busy airport, eat in a restaurant or sit in a medical waiting room before the telltale rash develops. By the time measles is diagnosed, hundreds or even thousands of people may have been exposed. An investigation published in The Journal of Infectious Diseases (Feb. 27, 2026) illustrates just how easily measles outbreaks can spread in the modern travel era.One infected traveler who arrived at Denver International Airport in May 2025 was ultimately connected with at least 17 cases in multiple states. Public health investigators had to evaluate approximately 1,400 potential contacts. A single passenger had set off a multistate public-health scramble. Measles Outbreaks Pose a New Problem: Who Will Recognize It? There is another reason today’s measles outbreaks are so difficult to contain: Most Americans—including many healthcare professionals—have never seen a case. Sixty or 80 years ago, a worried mother, an experienced family doctor or even a school nurse might have recognized measles almost immediately. The combination of a high fever, hacking cough, runny nose, red watery eyes and a rash spreading downward from the face would have been painfully familiar. Today, measles may be the last thing anyone suspects. Parents may assume their child has the flu, COVID, an allergic reaction or some other viral rash. A medical resident or emergency physician may have studied measles in a textbook but never encountered it in an exam room. Because measles became so uncommon in the United States, healthcare providers may have a low level of suspicion and delay the correct diagnosis. That delay can have serious consequences. A child with fever and cough may spend hours in a crowded waiting room before the rash is recognized. By then, patients, parents, nurses, receptionists and other staff members may have been exposed. Because measles virus can linger in the air for up to two hours, people may enter the room long after the sick child has left and still become infected. That is why anyone who suspects measles should call the doctor, urgent-care center or emergency department before arriving. The facility can arrange for the patient to enter without sitting in a common waiting area and can take appropriate isolation precautions. Healthcare professionals are advised to think of measles when a patient has fever and rash along with cough, runny nose or red eyes—especially after international travel, exposure to another person with a rash or residence in a community experiencing an outbreak. Once measles is suspected, the patient should be masked and isolated immediately. Clinicians should contact the local health department and collect samples for laboratory confirmation. A disease can become unfamiliar without becoming harmless. In fact, forgetting what measles looks like gives this extraordinarily contagious virus one more opportunity to spread. The Hidden Price of Measles Outbreaks Hospitalizations are only one part of the damage. When a student is exposed to measles, health departments may require an unvaccinated child to remain home for weeks. Parents miss work. Schools must identify contacts and review immunization records. Medical centers isolate suspected patients and trace everyone who may have shared the air. The major South Carolina outbreak that began in 2025 ultimately produced about 1,000 cases. Dozens of schools were affected, and hundreds of people were quarantined. For families, that can mean missed classes, lost wages, unexpected childcare problems and enormous anxiety. The Actual Price of Measles You could easily be paying for measles. Researchers at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health reviewed the cost of responding to measles outbreaks in 18 states. They estimated an average cost of approximately $43,000 for every measles case, although the figure ranged from under $7,000 to more than $243,000. Those estimates include medical care and public-health work such as laboratory testing, vaccination clinics, contact tracing and emergency response. They do not fully capture lost productivity, disrupted education, caregiver time or the lifelong costs of neurological injury. No one writes a $43,000 check when a child develops measles. The expense is scattered among hospitals, health departments, schools, employers, insurers, families and taxpayers. That makes the cost easy to overlook—but it will affect more people than is generally appreciated. Why Are Measles Outbreaks Returning? The United States did not eradicate measles in 2000. It eliminated endemic transmission. Elimination meant that measles was no longer circulating continuously inside the country. Travelers could still contract the virus abroad and bring it home. For many years, those imported infections usually fizzled out. The virus encountered too few susceptible people to keep spreading. That protective barrier is now developing big cracks. The CDC reports that MMR vaccination coverage among American kindergartners fell from 95.2 percent during the 2019-2020 school year to 92.5 percent during 2024-2025. That may sound like a small decline, but measles is so contagious that communities generally need vaccination rates around 95 percent to prevent sustained transmission. The national average also conceals large local pockets where vaccination rates are much lower. Once measles reaches one of those communities, an imported case can become an outbreak. Vaccines can become victims of their own success. When a disease disappears, parents no longer see children struggling to breathe, developing brain inflammation or dying from the infection. The risk from the vaccine may begin to feel more immediate than the disease it prevents. Eventually, vaccine coverage declines enough for the disease to return. Then everyone is reminded why the vaccine was developed in the first place. Are Immigrants Responsible for Measles Outbreaks? Whenever we report on measles, some readers blame immigrants. Measles certainly can enter the country through international travel. But the southern border has been closed for more than a year. And yet cases have climbed to unprecedented levels this year. The overwhelming majority of patients have been U.S. residents. The large wave of infections in 2025 began in Texas and New Mexico. Investigators traced many early cases to a tight-knit Mennonite community in West Texas, where vaccination rates were low. Similar patterns have been observed in some Amish and ultra-Orthodox Jewish communities, as well as certain other religious or cultural groups where vaccination is discouraged. Vaccine hesitancy has also grown in parts of the broader population where distrust of public health institutions has increased. Tourists can bring measles into the US and Americans who vacation, work or study abroad can also bring measles back with them. Once measles arrives, the key question is not the traveler’s citizenship. It is whether the virus encounters enough susceptible people to continue spreading. A wall cannot stop an airborne virus carried home by an American tourist. Does the MMR Vaccine Work to Stop Measles Outbreaks? One dose of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine is about 93 percent effective against measles. Two doses are about 97 percent effective. No vaccine works perfectly. A small number of fully vaccinated people will still become infected, especially during a large outbreak with intense exposure. The 2026 numbers, however, show a striking pattern: - 93 percent of patients were unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination history. - 3 percent had received one MMR dose. - 4 percent had received two doses. The existence of breakthrough cases does not demonstrate that vaccination is useless. It shows that a very effective vaccine is not an invisible force field. Seat belts do not prevent every automobile death either. That does not make driving without one a wise experiment. Do Adults Need Another Measles Vaccination? This is where advice can become confusing. People born before 1957 are generally presumed to have immunity because nearly everyone was exposed to measles before the vaccine era. Some adults vaccinated between 1963 and 1967 received an older killed-virus vaccine that did not provide adequate protection. People who know they received that vaccine—or who received an unknown type of measles vaccine before 1968—should discuss revaccination with a healthcare professional. Most people who received the modern live-virus MMR vaccine according to the recommended schedule are considered protected for life against measles. Routine boosters are not generally recommended simply because many years have passed. People at increased risk, including international travelers, healthcare workers, college students and those living in an outbreak area, may need two documented doses. A blood test can also look for evidence of measles immunity, although public-health guidance generally favors vaccination when records are unavailable and there is no medical contraindication. There is no known harm from receiving another MMR dose if someone is already immune. Because MMR is a live-virus vaccine, however, it is not appropriate for everyone. Pregnant women and some people with severely weakened immune systems should not receive it. Such individuals should consult a knowledgeable healthcare professional rather than making the decision on their own. Why Vaccines Matter to Me Personally Please permit a brief personal note. As a very young child, I was hospitalized with polio at the Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia. I was placed in traction in an isolation ward. My parents could not visit me. Children around me were dying. It was terrifying, and the experience left emotional scars that have lasted a lifetime. A few years later, I developed pertussis, better known as whooping cough. I was extremely ill for a long time. Even today, a respiratory infection can trigger severe coughing spells that sound frightening. People who never witnessed polio, diphtheria, measles or whooping cough may find such diseases hard to imagine. That is understandable. Vaccination made them largely invisible. But invisible is not the same as extinct. Viruses and bacteria do not surrender. They wait for an opportunity. What the Record Measles Outbreaks Are Telling Us Compared with heart disease, cancer or dementia, 2,318 measles cases may seem like a trivial number. That misses the warning. Measles is sending up a flare. It is telling us that the country’s protective immunization barrier is weakening. When vaccination coverage declines, measles is often one of the first diseases to break through because it spreads so easily. Pertussis and other preventable infections may follow. The return of measles is not merely a story about one childhood rash. It is evidence that the United States is moving backward against diseases it once brought under control. Public-health officials will not restore trust by scolding skeptical parents or pretending vaccines are completely risk-free. Families deserve honest information, respectful answers and acknowledgement of genuine uncertainties. But they also deserve the truth about the diseases. Measles Outbreaks Will Not Disappear on Their Own Unless confidence in the MMR vaccine improves and vaccination coverage remains strong, measles outbreaks will continue to flare and spread. You can learn more about vaccine effectiveness at this link. Vaccines are not perfect, and like any medical intervention they can produce side effects in some people. But the protection they provide has prevented enormous suffering from d...

Time
As of July 23, 2026, and with more than five months still remaining in the year (N4).
Location and population
United States, with 45 jurisdictions reporting cases and 69 percent of patients younger than 20 (N10).
Event
The CDC confirmed 2,318 measles cases in the United States in 2026, marking the largest number in 35 years (N3, N6).
Scale, impact and risk
There were 35 newly reported outbreaks, 151 hospitalizations, and 93 percent of cases linked to outbreaks. Nearly half of all cases occurred among children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent involved children under 5. No reported measles deaths in 2026, but three deaths were reported in 2025 (N10, N12, N14).
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC estimates that SSPE develops in roughly seven to 11 people for every 100,000 reported measles cases, and the risk may be higher for children infected before age 2. The final consequences of the 2026 outbreaks may not be fully visible until the 2030s (N50, N52).

新闻简报马里兰州蒙哥马利县卫生官员正在努力确保所有学龄儿童在秋季学期开始前完成疫苗接种,因美国及马里兰州的麻疹病例有所增加。截至7月底,美国今年的麻疹病例数已超过2025年全年总数,马里兰州今年报告了13例病例,其中包括东海岸上游地区的一例新发病例。该县卫生官员Kisha Davis博士指出,高疫苗接种率使麻疹传播相对受控。美国公共卫生协会首席执行官Georges Benjamin博士强调了麻疹病例的经济负担,每例管理费用可高达50,000美元,并敦促家长为子女接种疫苗。当前重点在于预防麻疹病毒传播,因其可通过疫苗预防且可能对公共卫生和资源造成重大影响。

蒙哥马利县卫生官员正在努力确保所有学龄儿童在开学前获得所需的 疫苗接种。这包括解决马里兰州报告的 麻疹 病例,这些病例与前几年相比有所增加。麻疹病毒 被提及为一个令人担忧的问题,其中一些病例与未接种疫苗的个人有关。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Md. measles cases part of Montgomery Co. push to vaccinate children before school starts - WTOP

News Brief
Montgomery County, Maryland, health officials are working to ensure all school-aged children are vaccinated before the fall semester begins, as measles cases have increased in the U.S. and Maryland. By late July, the U.S. had more measles cases than in all of 2025, with Maryland reporting 13 cases for the year, including a new one on the Upper Eastern Shore. Dr. Kisha Davis, the county’s health officer, noted that high vaccination rates have kept measles outbreaks relatively contained. Dr. Georges Benjamin, CEO of the American Public Health Association, highlighted the economic burden of measles cases, which can cost up to $50,000 each to manage, and urged parents to vaccinate their children. The focus is on preventing the spread of the measles virus, which is a concern due to its vaccine-preventable nature and potential impact on public health and resources.

Md. measles cases part of Montgomery Co. push to vaccinate children before school starts Kate Ryan | kryan@wtop.com August 6, 2026, 4:44 AM Share This: share on facebook share on X share on threads share on linkedin share on email print Before summer vacation even started, Montgomery County, Maryland, health officials were working to make sure all school-aged children have their required vaccinations before heading back to school this fall. Before summer vacation even started, Montgomery County, Maryland, health officials were working to make sure all school-aged children have their required vaccinations before heading back to school this fall. Related stories There are already more US measles cases this year than in all of 2025 Alexandria urges parents to avoid last-minute rush for school vaccines Virginia resident has state’s 1st locally acquired case of dengue fever And according to the latest data, vaccination rates are ahead of last year’s at this time, said Dr. Kisha Davis, Montgomery County’s health officer. “I am cautiously optimistic that our numbers of measles vaccinations and all school-age vaccines are good; are where they’re supposed to be,” she said Wednesday. That’s especially important, Davis said, as more measles cases are reported across the country and in Maryland. By late July, the U.S. had more reported cases of the measles than the 2,289 in 2025, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The measles virus in Maryland is relatively contained. This week, a new case on the Upper Eastern Shore, brought the state’s total to 13 for the year. “When we see cases in Maryland, and we do every year, we will see a few sporadic cases of measles that usually pop up outside of the county,” Davis said. “They don’t have anywhere to spread because our vaccination rates are so high, and that’s why we are not seeing the outbreaks that we’re seeing in other states because we’ve maintained such a high vaccination rate.” Davis spoke during Montgomery County Executive Marc Elrich’s weekly briefing and was joined by Dr. Georges Benjamin, the CEO of the American Public Health Association. “We’ve got good public health officials, a good system in Maryland,” Benjamin said, “and I encourage people to make sure, particularly now as they’re getting ready to plan going back to school, for parents to get all their kids vaccinated.” Benjamin said that some of the reported measles cases occurred among people who were unvaccinated. “Being unvaccinated puts you at an extraordinary risk of (contracting) vaccine-preventable diseases,” he said, “and the key word is vaccine-preventable diseases.” And every case is a “tragedy,” Benjamin said. “This is a disease you don’t want to get. You get really, really sick when you get measles,” he said. Measles outbreaks also carry a high economic cost, forcing state and local governments to spend as much as $50,000 to manage each case, Benjamin said. “That’s money that could be used for education. That’s dollars that can be used for our roads. That’s dollars that can, you know, be used for other things that the county council and the county executive and the citizens of our county believe are most important,” he said. CDC 在扩大的美国范围内增加了近 100 个新病例 麻疹 暴发 - CIDRAP

CDC adds almost 100 new cases to expanding US measles outbreak - CIDRAP
新闻简报The CDC has added nearly 100 new cases to an expanding measles outbreak in the United States, as reported by CIDRAP on August 7, 2026. This update highlights the ongoing nature of the outbreak, which has been growing and shows no signs of slowing down. The outbreak's expansion raises concerns about the potential loss of the US's measles elimination status, which has been maintained for 26 years. An expert panel is set to review the data to determine the current status of measles in the country. The outbreak's impact includes significant costs, as seen in the 2025 outbreak in New Mexico, which cost $5.4 million. Public health agencies are working to contain the spread, but the situation remains unresolved, with further investigation needed to assess the full scope and risk of the outbreak.

Lehigh Valley confirms 1st measles case amid nation’s worst year for virus in decades

Lehigh Valley confirms 1st measles case amid nation’s worst year for virus in decades
新闻简报Lehigh Valley Health Network 确认了该地区首例 麻疹 病例,这是全国感染激增的一部分,这使得 2026 年成为自 1991 年以来 麻疹 最糟糕的一年。该病例涉及 Lehigh 县的两名儿科患者,邻近县报告了另外 7 例病例。卫生官员指出,2026 年 7 月的 麻疹 病例数超过了 2025 年全年的病例总数,凸显了人们日益增长的担忧。卢克大学健康网络的儿科医生詹妮弗·詹科博士强调了该病毒的高度传染性及其对未接种疫苗的个体构成的风险,并表示 90% 的没有保护的接触者都会感染这种疾病。截至 7 月 23 日,疾病控制与预防中心报告了 2,318 例 麻疹 病例,超过 2025 年的总数 2,289 例。卫生官员敦促采取 疫苗接种,特别是对于可能没有得到足够保护的儿童和成人。Lehigh Valley Health Network 已采取预防措施,并与州卫生官员密切合作来管理 暴发。该情况仍在积极调查中,尚未有解决的迹象。

Lehigh Valley confirms 1st measles case amid nation’s worst year for virus in decades

News Brief
Lehigh Valley Health Network confirmed the first measles case in the region, part of a national surge in infections that has made 2026 the worst year for measles since 1991. The case involves two pediatric patients in Lehigh County, with seven additional cases reported in neighboring counties. Health officials have noted that measles cases in July 2026 exceeded the total for all of 2025, highlighting a growing concern. Dr. Jennifer Janco, a pediatrician at Luke's University Health Network, emphasized the virus's high contagiousness and the risk it poses to unvaccinated individuals, stating that 90% of those exposed without protection will contract the disease. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported 2,318 measles cases as of July 23, surpassing the 2025 total of 2,289. Health officials are urging vaccination, particularly for children and adults who may not have received adequate protection. Lehigh Valley Health Network has implemented precautions and is working closely with state health officials to manage the outbreak. The situation remains under active investigation, with no indication of resolution yet.
Original source text (English)
Health officials this week confirmed the Lehigh Valley's first measles case amid a rise in infections across Pennsylvania and the United States. St. Luke's University Health Network announced Monday night it is treating its first case of the highly contagious disease in recent memory. It is a pediatric case, the network said. "Measles cases are on the rise in the United States, but we now have our first case here in the region," Dr. Jennifer Janco, a St. Luke's pediatrician, said in a video update released by the network . St. Luke's spokesman Sam Kennedy clarified Tuesday the network is treating two patients with measles, both pediatric cases in Lehigh County of siblings who tested positive. One was receiving inpatient treatment, and the other was recovering at home. The infections come as Pennsylvania experiences an increase in measles cases , bringing a national health concern to local communities. Health officials are now urging residents to check their vaccination status and get vaccinated if needed. Janco, chairwoman of the St. Luke's Pediatric Department, emphasized the serious nature of the virus. "Measles is one of the most contagious diseases that we know of. You only need to be around the airspace, not the person, and it can linger in the air," she explained. The virus poses a significant threat to unprotected individuals, Janco said: "Nine out of 10 people who aren't protected are going to contract measles if they're exposed." The disease can lead to severe health complications requiring hospitalization. Janco warned that measles can cause swelling of the brain and affect the lungs. The risks extend beyond immediate illness, as the virus can create ongoing health problems for survivors. The number of measles cases in the U.S. in July surpassed the record-breaking tally for all of 2025 , with five more months of 2026 still to come. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said it logged 2,318 cases of measles as of July 23. The nation had 2,289 cases in 2025, the worst year for measles in more than three decades. Now, 2026 has easily become the U.S.'s worst year for measles since 1991, as major outbreaks in South Carolina, Utah and Arizona have sickened hundreds and smaller clusters of cases sparked in other states. A Pennsylvania Department of Health dashboard of measles infections statewide shows 176 cases as of Monday, including the two in Lehigh County and seven in neighboring counties — three in Montgomery and four in Berks. Despite concerns of an outbreak, health officials stress that measles is preventable through vaccination. The cases are affecting children in the region, but Janco pointed to an effective solution. "The good news is we can prevent measles cases. So, yes, we are seeing cases in children, but we have a very powerful prevention tool in the form of vaccination," she said. St. Luke's advises parents with unvaccinated children to contact their pediatrician immediately to schedule vaccinations. Many adults may also need protection, and the health network urges certain adult populations to verify their vaccination status. "People born between 1963 and 1967 may have had an older version of the vaccine and might benefit from today's updated version," Janco explained. Additionally, adults vaccinated during childhood may not have received adequate protection. "If you were vaccinated before 1991, you may have only received one dose because that's what the recommendation was then," she said. The current medical recommendation calls for two doses of the measles vaccine. Janco stressed the effectiveness of the complete vaccination series. "We know two doses of the vaccine is a powerful protection tool and we are not seeing cases of measles in vaccinated persons," she said. Those unsure about their vaccination status should consult with their healthcare provider to determine if they need vaccination or additional doses, health officials stressed. Lehigh Valley Health Network, part of Jefferson Health , is also on the alert for measles, with a spokesperson telling lehighvalleylive.com: "The Pennsylvania Department of Health notified us of two confirmed cases of measles locally, following the evaluation of a patient at Lehigh Valley Reilly Children's Hospital. "Appropriate precautions were implemented as soon as measles was suspected, and we will continue to work closely with state health officials." The Associated Press contributed to this report. B routine priorityPublic-health news

I’m a Doctor Who Treated Measles in the 2015 Outbreak. 7 Things People Keep Getting Wrong - AOL.com

News Brief
This article, published on August 4, 2026, by AOL.com, discusses the resurgence of measles in the U.S. and the author's personal experience treating patients during the 2014-2015 Disneyland outbreak, which spread to six states and Canada. The author highlights the challenges in diagnosing measles, as it often presents with non-specific symptoms that can be mistaken for other illnesses. The CDC reported that measles cases in 2026 exceeded those of 2025, raising concerns about the U.S. losing its measles-free status. The article outlines the severe complications associated with measles, including hospitalization, pneumonia, encephalitis, and death, particularly in vulnerable groups like children under 5, adults over 20, and immunocompromised individuals. It also explains that while the measles vaccine is not a perfect solution, it provides lifelong protection in 97% of cases and is crucial in preventing transmission. The author stresses the importance of early diagnosis and the role of vitamin A as a supportive treatment, though it is not a cure. The article serves as a reminder of the dangers of measles and the need for continued vaccination efforts.
Original source text (English)
When treating measles patients infected in the Disneyland outbreak that spread to six states and Canada between 2014 and 2015, I learned firsthand that even “the happiest place on Earth” is not immune to a preventable crisis. In fact, many of the urgent public health lessons I took away from this period feel just as relevant as the U.S. battles a 35-year high in measles infections. Last week, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported the number of measles cases in 2026 had surpassed 2025’s total, and the nation’s status of having eliminated the virus is at risk, NBC News reported . The chaos that followed the Disneyland outbreak was overwhelming, and the fear of a localized outbreak within the facility where I worked was terrifying. Measles is one of the most contagious viruses on Earth and can cause severe complications. Over a decade later, the short-lived “Disneyland effect” — which temporarily boosted public support for vaccinations — has faded from our collective memory, though it remains vivid in mine. As we face this resurgence, here’s what I want people to know about the measles virus. Your Doctor Probably Has Never Seen a Case of Measles I trained to be an infectious disease specialist. Yet, despite nine years of rigorous medical training, I never encountered a measles case until I was thrown into an outbreak years after graduating. And I’m not alone. With 1 million active physicians in the U.S., the odds of any individual clinician encountering the disease have been incredibly low over the past two decades. Following measles’ U.S. elimination in 2000, the country averaged 100-200 cases annually between 2001 and 2019 (outside of major outbreaks). Research shows this lack of firsthand exposure has created knowledge gaps that could delay making a measles diagnosis. And early diagnosis is important to prevent further spread and complications. Measles Is Not Easy to Diagnose Measles typically begins with non-specific, flu-like symptoms that are easily dismissed as a standard cold until the hallmark rash appears. In medical school, we are taught the “3 Cs” — cough, coryza (runny nose) and conjunctivitis (red eyes) — followed by a blotchy red rash that starts on the face and spreads downward . But when I encountered my first real patient, I almost missed the diagnosis. The only reason I tested for measles was my patient asked me to. Patients are sometimes their best advocate, so don’t be afraid to ask your doctor to order a test or think of diagnosis they may have missed. In its early stages, measles closely resembles many other illnesses. Even a routine allergic reaction to medication can cause a drug rash that looks virtually identical to measles to an untrained eye. Measles Is Not Just a Childhood Rash Measles is a highly contagious disease that can cause ear infections, diarrhea and severe complications in 30% of cases , including: Hospitalization : One in five unvaccinated U.S. patients end up hospitalized. Pneumonia : One out of 20 children develop a lung infection. Brain Swelling : One out of 1,000 children suffer from encephalitis (which can lead to deafness and intellectual disability). Death : The virus kills one to three out of every 1,000 children infected. (There are no documented cases of a healthy, vaccinated person dying of measles.) The risk is highest in children less than 5, adults older than 20, pregnant women, immunocompromised individuals and those with vitamin A deficiency . Measles can also cause complications after recovery. A rare and often fatal central nervous system disease called subacute sclerosing panencephalitis can strike seven to 10 years after the initial infection. Infants under 2 face the highest risk. You Can Still Get Measles If You Are Fully Vaccinated Two doses of the MMR vaccine provide lifelong protection in 97% of people, according to the CDC . The remaining 3% are susceptible, usually because because their immune systems fail to produce enough antibodies, or intense, prolonged exposure overwhelms their defenses. (Think of living in a confined household where multiple unvaccinated individuals are actively shedding the virus.) But vaccinated individuals who experience breakthrough infections usually suffer a much milder symptoms and are much less likely to transmit the virus to others, research shows . Measles is one of the most contagious viral pathogens on Earth, capable of living in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves the room, so if you suspect you have measles, always call a health care facility before showing up so they can take proper isolation protocols. The Measles Vaccine Does Not Cause Person-to-Person Spread The measles vaccine uses a weakened version of the actual virus to safely mimic a natural infection, which results in long-lasting immunity. (It should not be used in immunocompromised people.) I’ve often been asked if a vaccinated person can shed the virus from the vaccine and endanger others, especially immunocompromised folks. Fortunately, the vaccine strain of measles cannot spread from person to person, per the CDC . Vitamin A Is a Supportive Treatment, Not a Cure-All I remember having to tell a measles patient’s mother during the 2015 outbreak that there is no approved antiviral cure, a humbling reminder of our clinical limits. Treatment is strictly supportive: acetaminophen or ibuprofen for fever and pain, aggressive hydration and lots of rest. Vitamin A can also be a supportive treatment, as the measles virus rapidly depletes levels of the nutrient in the blood. During the measles outbreak in Texas last year, the CDC said vitamin A should be given to children hospitalized with severe symptoms. At the same time, the agency also stressed that vitamin A does not prevent infection and is not a replacement for the vaccine. Giving vitamin A also necessitates oversight from a health care provider, since vitamin A toxicity comes with severe risks. The Measles Virus Is More Dangerous Than the Vaccine, Data Show Many patients asked me during the 2015 outbreak if the measles vaccine is safe, and I told them the dangers of catching measles are much greater than the side effects of the vaccine. There are no documented deaths in healthy people due to the measles vaccine, whereas up to three children out of every 1,000 infected with measles will die, according to the Infectious Diseases Society of America . The vaccine can very rarely cause fever-induced seizures — which are not associated with long-term health effects, per the CDC — and immune thrombocytopenic purpura, a rare condition that disrupts blood clotting. ITP is usually not life-threatening and occurs in about one out of 40,000 MMR vaccinations. A measles infection causes severe complications much more often: According to the CDC , about one in 20 children will develop pneumonia and one in 1,000 will develop dangerous swelling of the brain. This article was originally published on TODAY.com
