The article discusses the general U.S. population and highlights risks for children under 5, adults over 20, pregnant women, immunocompromised individuals, and those with vitamin A deficiency.
Event
The article describes the author's experience treating measles patients during the 2015 Disneyland outbreak and discusses the current resurgence of measles in the U.S. It also outlines the challenges in diagnosing measles and the risks associated with the disease.
Scale, impact and risk
The CDC reported that measles cases in 2026 surpassed 2025’s total, indicating a significant resurgence. The article highlights that measles can cause severe complications, including hospitalization in 1 in 5 unvaccinated children, pneumonia in 1 in 20, encephalitis in 1 in 1,000, and death in 1-3 out of every 1,000 children infected. It also notes that the U.S. had eliminated measles in 2000 but is now at risk of losing that status.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC and other health authorities are involved in monitoring and responding to the measles resurgence. The article emphasizes the importance of early diagnosis, isolation protocols, and the role of the measles vaccine in preventing transmission. It also mentions the use of vitamin A as a supportive treatment, though it is not a cure.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

随着 麻疹 案件的增加,美国官员专注于识别和快速行动 - 《卫报》

US officials focus on identification and rapid action as measles cases rise - The Guardian
新闻简报文章报告称,美国 麻疹 病例大幅增加,截至 2026 年 7 月底确诊感染人数为 2,371 例,为 30 年来最高。暴发 正在亚利桑那州、南卡罗来纳州和犹他州发生,特拉华州出现了五起聚集性病例。特拉华州的四例病例涉及肯特县未接种疫苗的男性,第五例是症状轻微的已接种疫苗的个体。官员们强调快速识别和采取行动,包括 疫苗接种 驱动器和接触者追踪,以遏制传播。疫苗接种 率的下降,特别是在幼儿园儿童中,已从 2016 年的 98.5% 下降到 2026 年的 94.1%,低于群体免疫所需的 95% 阈值。公共卫生专家强调了教育和获得医疗保健对于那些需要 疫苗 的人的重要性。回应包括努力解决因错误信息而加剧的 疫苗 犹豫,包括将自闭症与 疫苗接种 联系起来的说法以及认为儿童接受了太多 疫苗 的信念。目前,官员们不建议一岁以下或四岁以下的儿童尽早接受 疫苗接种,除非他们接触过阳性病例。文章强调了 麻疹 的可预防性以及持续采取公共卫生措施的必要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至2026年7月底,美国已超过去年麻疹病例总数,确诊感染人数达到2,371例。特拉华州的暴发疫情始于2026年7月首例病例的公布,两天后当地宣布进入暴发状态。该文章发布于2026-08-03。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在亚利桑那州、南卡罗来纳州和犹他州,特别关注特拉华州。在特拉华州,前四名患者是肯特县未接种疫苗的男性,第五名患者是接种疫苗的人。受影响人群包括未接种疫苗的个体和 疫苗接种 率较低的人群,特别是幼儿园儿童,其比率从 2016 年的 98.5% 下降到 2026 年的 94.1%。
事件
随着 麻疹 病例的增加,美国官员正在重点关注识别和快速行动。特拉华州官员宣布有 5 例已知病例为 暴发,其中包括 4 名未接种疫苗的男性和一名症状轻微的接种疫苗者。文章报道,麻疹 暴发 在美国各地不断增加,病例数达到 30 年来最高。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 7 月,美国已确诊 2,371 例 麻疹 感染,为 30 年来最高。暴发 集中在亚利桑那州、南卡罗来纳州和犹他州,特拉华州出现了 5 起聚集性病例。由于 疫苗接种 比率下降,暴发 传播的风险加大,该比率已降至群体免疫所需的 95% 阈值以下。
应对、状态与不确定性
特拉华州官员对 暴发 做出了迅速响应,实施了 疫苗接种 驱动器和接触者追踪。公共卫生官员不建议一岁以下或四岁以下的儿童尽早接受 疫苗接种,除非他们接触过阳性病例。应对措施包括为家庭提供教育,并为需要 疫苗 的人提供医疗服务。文章指出,暴发 是通过接触者追踪确定的,由于医疗隐私,有关病例的详细信息有限。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

US officials focus on identification and rapid action as measles cases rise - The Guardian

News Brief
The article reports a significant rise in measles cases in the United States, with 2,371 confirmed infections by the end of July 2026, the highest in 30 years. Outbreaks are occurring in Arizona, South Carolina, and Utah, with Delaware experiencing a cluster of five cases. Four of the Delaware cases involved unvaccinated men in Kent county, while the fifth was a vaccinated individual with mild symptoms. Officials are emphasizing rapid identification and action, including vaccination drives and contact tracing, to contain the spread. The decline in vaccination rates, particularly among kindergartners, has dropped from 98.5% in 2016 to 94.1% in 2026, below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity. Public health experts highlight the importance of education and access to healthcare for those needing vaccines. The response includes efforts to address vaccine hesitancy, which has been fueled by misinformation, including claims linking autism to vaccination and the belief that children receive too many vaccines. Officials are not currently recommending early vaccination for children under one or under four unless they are in contact with a positive case. The article underscores the preventable nature of measles and the need for continued public health measures.

Photograph: The Washington Post/Getty Images View image in fullscreen A doctor assesses a patient with measles symptoms in Spartanburg, South Carolina. Photograph: The Washington Post/Getty Images US officials focus on identification and rapid action as measles cases rise Authorities in Delaware tackle cluster with vaccination and contact tracing – but field has been weakened by cuts Measles outbreaks are igniting throughout the United States as the nation passes the highest case count in 30 years – putting greater pressure on state and local health departments and highlighting the importance of early identification and quick action. The US has officially surpassed last year’s tally of measles cases, reaching 2,371 confirmed infections by the end of July. Those numbers have been driven by clusters in Arizona, South Carolina, and Utah – but in times of high transmission, outbreaks elsewhere become more likely. And with decreasing vaccination rates across the country, these sparks are falling on increasingly dry timber. In July, Delaware announced its first case of measles in more than a decade. Two days later, officials declared an outbreak, with five known cases. Like other parts of the country, vaccination rates have dropped in Delaware, from 98.5% of kindergartners vaccinated against measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) in 2016 compared to 94.1% now. A threshold of about 95% is considered essential for herd immunity to protect those who cannot get vaccinated or who don’t mount a good immune reaction – including babies and cancer patients. “We don’t want to be below that threshold. And the lower we get, the more likelihood there is that someone who can’t be vaccinated yet – because of their age or because of their immune status – is going to come into contact with someone who has unfortunately contracted measles,” said Robert Rosenbaum, Delaware’s medical director for emergency medical services and preparedness. Attitudes toward vaccines have changed in a couple of different ways, he said. The idea of a link between autism and vaccination, now disproved by multiple studies, proved to be “horrible misinformation” that “created a lot of confusion for people”, Rosenbaum said. Some parents also began trying to create their own vaccination schedule out of the belief that children receive too many vaccines – a view frequently espoused by Donald Trump. The pandemic supercharged existing misinformation about vaccines. “There were a lot of people who had feelings about the Covid vaccination, and that has crossed over to other vaccinations where people are moving away from everything,” Rosenbaum said. William Chasanov, an infectious disease physician at Beebe Healthcare in Lewes, Delaware, said he “did see the pendulum swing with Covid”. The Covid vaccines saved hundreds of thousands of lives – but at the same time, more of the public began seeing vaccines as optional, he said. “There was a shift in thought that ‘maybe I don’t need vaccines for things that I haven’t seen,’” Chasanov said. The measles vaccine has in some ways become a victim of its own success; those who haven’t encountered measles before don’t understand why it needs to be prevented, he said. Robert F Kennedy Jr, now the health secretary, spent about two decades spreading misinformation about vaccines – and that advocacy went into overtime during the pandemic, when wider audiences were exposed to his anti-vaccine views. As health secretary, Kennedy has framed measles vaccination during this outbreak as a personal choice, and he has falsely claimed it’s “very difficult” for healthy children to die of measles. But the evidence strongly points to the effectiveness and safety of two doses of the MMR vaccines, Rosenbaum said. “Anyone who isn’t advocating that is not following science consistently.” Vaccines are key to prevent measles – but equally important is education for families who have questions about vaccines and access to healthcare for those who need the shots and other care, experts said. Measles is an awful disease, Chasanov said. It can bring high fevers, difficulty breathing, and extreme lethargy. It can infect the brain, causing encephalitis, which can have lasting health effects. And it can lead to death – even, sometimes, years later. “Understanding what the consequences could be is part of that education,” Chasanov said. “And it’s important to know that the measles vaccine is one of the most highly effective vaccines that we’ve had on the market for decades ... Measles is highly contagious, but it’s also highly preventable.” The day after the first case was announced in Delaware, more than 60 people received MMR vaccinations at public health clinics and mobile vaccination units. “When things hit closer to home, you start asking questions because it becomes more personal,” Chasanov said. “It spurs a healthy conversation that I encourage people to have with their clinicians to say, ‘Do I need to do anything different with vaccination?’” Anyone born before 1989 may need to get a second dose of the measles shot, which became standard practice in the late 80s. One measles shot is about 93% effective, but two are about 97% effective. Those born before 1957 likely have immunity from measles infection as a child. At this point, public health officials aren’t recommending early vaccination for children under one or children under four who have only received one shot – unless they’re in contact with a positive patient. “What we don’t have at this point is a large-scale epidemic where we might be changing some of those vaccine recommendations,” Rosenbaum said. “Certainly, if the numbers change or for some indication to change it, we would revisit that.” Delaware has released few details about the cases for medical privacy reasons. The first four patients were all unvaccinated men in Kent county. The fifth patient was vaccinated and has extremely mild symptoms – one of the benefits of vaccination. An outbreak is declared when three or more connected cases appear. “Delaware has responded very rapidly,” Chasanov said. The division of public health met with healthcare systems and providers to walk through plans in case someone with measles walks through their doors. Officials are approaching the outbreak with a two-pronged approach: vaccination and contact tracing, the mainstays of public health. The field, however, has been battered – by the long hours and stress of the pandemic, and by chronic underfunding and growing threats that stretch public health thin. Contact tracing is how officials discovered the four additional cases. After the first person tested positive, public health staff got in touch with anyone who was in contact with him – searching especially for very high-risk people like young children (and anyone else) not vaccinated yet or only partially immunized, pregnant people, and anyone with a weakened immune response. “The contact tracing has really done a nice job of identifying the at-risk contacts,” Rosenbaum said. “So the good news is, I would say, if people haven’t heard from us, we’re not actively looking for them.” Now, they’re monitoring contacts to see if they develop symptoms, and they’re looking for any other cases they may have missed in contact tracing. They’re also trying to reach anyone with poor access to healthcare, including those without health insurance. ”The public health safety net is an important one,” Rosenbaum said. “We don’t want anyone who wants to get vaccinated to feel like they’re unable to do so because of insurance status ... That’s the function of public health, right? To help people who are at risk and may be vulnerable.” Outbreaks like these are happening all over the country, with pockets of cases sprouting up and officials racing to prevent spread through early identification, contact tracing, vaccination, education, and improved healthcare access. “We have a process that we follow,” Rosenbaum said. “And when we follow that process well, we have the opportunity to stem the outbreak.” Explore more on these topics US healthcare news Share Reuse this content Most viewed California woman who admitted to stabbing wife had also killed husband Trump’s favorite cable news channel is pushing back against his war on Iran ‘It didn’t need to end this way’: mother of US teen trafficked and killed decries police failures ‘Not afraid of controversy’: Edinburgh fringe rejects calls to cancel Amanda Knox comedy Cambridge academics reject university’s attempt to end row over Jason Arday Most viewed Most viewed

B routine priorityPublic-health news

St. Luke's Health Network reports its first measles cases 'in recent memory' - pottsmerc.com

News Brief
St. Luke's Health Network in Pennsylvania reported its first measles cases 'in recent memory,' involving two unvaccinated siblings from Lehigh County. These cases are part of a broader measles outbreak in eastern Pennsylvania that began in late April 2026, with 75 infections linked to the initial outbreak and 176 confirmed cases statewide in 2026. The outbreak has prompted public health recommendations for early vaccination, particularly for infants. The two children are recovering at home, and contact tracing has been completed by both the health network and the Pennsylvania Department of Health. The outbreak highlights the resurgence of measles in the region, with the last known cases in Lehigh Valley dating back to 2019. The measles virus is highly contagious, and the outbreak has raised concerns about public health and vaccination rates.
Original source text (English)
A 3D model shows a visualization of the measles virus. (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention/TNS) By Leif Greiss | lgreiss@mcall.com | The Morning Call PUBLISHED: August 3, 2026 at 7:52 PM EDT | UPDATED: August 6, 2026 at 3:06 PM EDT Getting your Trinity Audio player ready... St. Luke’s University Hospital said late Monday that it’s treating its first cases of the measles “in recent memory.” According to a health network spokesperson, the hospital was treating two children from Lehigh County. The two are siblings, and both were unvaccinated, the spokesperson said. Though both are St. Luke’s patients, one child at first visited a Lehigh Valley Health Network hospital while St. Luke’s pediatricians attended to the other child remotely. The network did not specify where in Lehigh County the siblings were from. Dr. Jennifer Janco, chair of pediatrics at St. Luke’s, said one was hospitalized but released, and both are recovering at home. Janco said the network already completed contact tracing for its networks and that there were no potential exposures at any St. Luke’s facilities. All other contract tracing is being conducted by the Pennsylvania Department of Health. Janco said that the children had traveled internationally and there were multiple potential places where they could have come in contact with measles. St. Luke’s last month warned of an eastern Pennsylvania outbreak of the highly infectious disease and encouraged parents to take extra steps to protect their children. Seventy-five infections were linked to an outbreak that began in Lebanon and Lancaster counties in late April. Two cases were reported in Berks County, according to the Pennsylvania Department of Health. Across the state, so far there have been 176 confirmed cases of measles in 2026, with 35 reported in the last seven days. Because of the outbreak, the Health Department is recommending infants ages 6-11 months receive an early dose of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine. Children who have already received one MMR dose at 1 year of age, which is the typical age the first doses are given, may be eligible to receive their second dose earlier than their fourth year, which is the usual time the second dose is given. “Measles is one of the most contagious infectious diseases, but vaccination is the safest and most effective way to protect your child and those around them,” Janco said. The St. Luke’s measles cases are the first known ones reported in the Lehigh Valley since 2019 . An Allentown resident at Lehigh Valley Hospital-Cedar Crest with measles marked the city’s first case in more than 20 years, officials said at the time. It caused five people who were potentially exposed to be ordered to quarantine, including one who received a rare court order to do so. There were two potential measles exposures in 2011, according to previous reporting by The Morning Call. Since the introduction and adoption of the measles vaccine in the 1960s, measles cases in the Lehigh Valley have been rare. The last known death from measles in the Lehigh Valley was a Bethlehem Township girl who died in 1991 after she contracted the disease in Philadelphia amid a major national outbreak. Later that year, six children in the Lehigh Valley got measles, as part of an outbreak that affected at least 14 children in Lehigh, Northampton, Lancaster and Lebanon counties. Before the introduction of the vaccine, measles was a serious and pervasive threat that often seriously disrupted school and everyday life. In 1912, The Morning Call reported that one child being sent to school sick with measles resulted in 12 Lehigh Valley schools temporarily closing and 14,000 students missing school to prevent exposure amid a national measles outbreak. In winter 1916, another national outbreak led to hundreds of people in Allentown getting infected with the disease and the entire city being placed under quarantine, with all schools shut down for several weeks. Yet another national epidemic of measles in 1923 led to at least 500 people in Allentown getting sick in January, February and March, and on one day in May 1923, there were 64 school children in Alburtis, nearly a third of the students in the town, who were out sick with measles. Update: LVHN was also initially involved in the treatment of one child. Oral Antiviral GHP-88310 Blocks Measles-Like Transmission in Ferrets - Contagion Live

大肠杆菌食品安全李斯特菌沙门氏菌真菌感染耳念珠菌胃肠道感染C.
事件
据 Contagion Live 报道,一项研究发现,口服 抗病毒药物 GHP-88310 可以阻断雪貂体内的 麻疹 样 传播。
规模、影响与风险
该研究表明 GHP-88310 有可能预防雪貂中类似 麻疹 的 传播,但所提供的证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露数量。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是正在进行的研究的一部分,研究结果是初步的。证据中没有明确提及调查、测试、隔离或接触者追踪。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Oral Antiviral GHP-88310 Blocks Measles-Like Transmission in Ferrets - Contagion Live

News Brief
The news report from Contagion Live highlights a study on the oral antiviral GHP-88310, which shows potential to block measles-like transmission in ferrets. Published on August 3, 2026, the report notes that this finding is part of ongoing research and does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers. The study is considered preliminary, and no details on investigation, testing, isolation, or contact tracing are included. The report emphasizes the importance of further research to understand the implications of this antiviral for measles prevention, but it does not confirm any real-world outbreak or public health impact. The evidence is sourced from a general news section, indicating that the findings are not yet fully validated or applied in human populations.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

我和我的同事在评估美国 麻疹 消除状况时将关注什么

What my colleagues and I will look for as we assess U.S. measles elimination status
新闻简报本文讨论了美国国家核查委员会对该国 2026 年 麻疹 消除状态的评估。该委员会(包括作者和同事)正在审查数据,以确定美国是否保持了消除状态,还是有失去消除状态的风险。此次审查包括审查 麻疹 病例数(该数字已超过 2025 年),并分析了大多数州的 34 个 麻疹,其中犹他州的 暴发 持续了一年多。委员会还将考虑整个 基因组 测序数据,这为 传播 链提供了更好的分辨率。自 2025 年 1 月以来,美国每周都会报告 麻疹 病例,引发了人们对该病毒再次流行的担忧。泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 将于 2026 年 11 月做出消除状况的最终决定。报告内容属于机密,结果仍不确定。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026-08-06。活动日期为 2026 年 8 月中旬,自 2025 年 1 月以来持续存在 麻疹 案件。
地点与对象
是该地点,大多数州都报告了 麻疹 病例,包括南卡罗来纳州、犹他州和德克萨斯州。受影响人群包括接触 麻疹 的个人和感染该病毒的人。
事件
文章讨论了一个委员会对美国 麻疹 消除状态的评估,该委员会将审查数据以确定美国是否保持消除状态或处于失去消除状态的边缘。该活动是美国国家验证委员会针对消除 麻疹、风疹和先天性风疹综合症的审查过程。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年8月中旬,美国今年已报告2,371例麻疹病例,超过2025年全年。今年新增34例麻疹,其中犹他州的麻疹持续了一年多。风险在于,由于 传播 的持续进行以及该病毒有可能再次流行,美国可能会失去 麻疹 消除状态。
应对、状态与不确定性
国家核查委员会正在审查2026年麻疹消除报告,最终决定将由泛美卫生组织区域核查委员会于2026年11月做出。该报告包括完整的基因组测序数据,可能会提供更清晰的信息。结果是不确定的,保密协议阻止分享报告的内容。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

What my colleagues and I will look for as we assess U.S. measles elimination status

News Brief
This article discusses the U.S. National Verification Committee's assessment of the country's measles elimination status in 2026. The committee, which includes the author and colleagues, is reviewing data to determine if the U.S. has maintained its elimination status or is at risk of losing it. The review includes examining measles case numbers, which have already exceeded those of 2025, and analyzing 34 outbreaks across most states, with Utah's outbreak lasting over a year. The committee will also consider whole genome sequencing data, which offers better resolution of transmission chains. The U.S. has reported measles cases every week since January 2025, raising concerns about the virus becoming endemic again. The final decision on elimination status will be made by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) in November 2026. The report's contents are confidential, and the outcome remains uncertain.
Original source text (English)

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword Start solving OpinionFirst Opinion What my colleagues and I will look for as we assess U.S. measles elimination status Determining measles’ status in the U.S. is about more than just case numbers Manage alerts for this article Email this article Share this article A single virus particle, or “virion,” of measles virus, seen through a thin-section transmission electron micrograph (TEM).Cynthia S. Goldsmith, William Bellini/CDC By Noel T. Brewer Aug. 6, 2026 Brewer chairs the U.S. committee that reviews the annual CDC measles reports to verify whether the U.S. maintains or loses measles elimination status. Elimination of measles in the United States in 2000 was a landmark public health achievement. Twenty-five years later, the country has already reported more cases this year than it did in all of 2025, a stark reminder that public health victories can be reversed. In mid-August, colleagues and I will review the evidence and decide whether the U.S. has maintained measles elimination. In many ways, our roles bookend that history. Several of these colleagues helped lead the national effort that ended endemic measles transmission in 2000. And I now chair the committee that must determine whether that hard‐won status still holds — or whether the country is on the verge of losing it. Advertisement To independently certify the CDC’s annual report on measles elimination, the U.S. maintains the United States of America National Verification Committee for the Elimination of Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome. (Yes, it’s a mouthful.) After our committee’s review, we will forward the report and our expert opinion to the Regional Verification Commission of the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO). Then, in November, this PAHO committee will announce whether the U.S. has maintained measles elimination. Confidentiality agreements mean that I cannot share the contents of the 2026 report. But here’s what we will be looking for. A lot will be familiar. We will review measles rates for the nation. These data are public and show a runaway train. This year is about to exceed any previous year in the last third of a century, with 2,371 measles cases so far. Outbreaks are occurring in most states, with 34 new outbreaks this year alone. Utah’s outbreak, which began in June 2025, has lasted for more than a year so far. Advertisement We will look at detailed analyses of each outbreak. The largest outbreaks for the report period have spanned the continental U.S. — South Carolina and Utah, followed by Texas and Virginia. One of the most worrisome is the ongoing outbreak in Utah, which apparently has gone on for more than 12 months, the main criterion for loss of elimination. Importantly, we will look at how long the chains of transmission lasted. This is usually established with shoe-leather epidemiology. When measles cases are reported, public health workers fan out in the community to interview the various contacts and identify places they’ve been and may have exposed others. They use this narrative information to stitch together chains of transmission. They also try to prevent further cases by offering postexposure vaccination or immune globulin and quarantining people who were exposed to others with measles. What will be new in this year’s report is whole genome sequencing. In recent years, CDC had done partial genome sequencing to look at the genetic signature of the measles viruses causing the cases and try to understand their evolution, which was the best that they could do. But the technology has advanced to be more comprehensive and detailed. Whole genome sequencing allows much better resolution of transmission chains. Several states, including Utah, Arizona, and South Carolina, have already taken this approach. They uncovered signatures of unseen cases by noticing abrupt “skips” in the virus’s evolutionary trail. By relying on a central lab and standardized methods, the CDC can now track measles across state lines with far greater precision. In 2025, Canada and the U.S. used partial genome sequencing data to verify their measles elimination status. For Canada, the data were especially important. Seasonal workers brought measles from Mexico, and new cases left with them in the fall and returned with them in the spring. Partial genome sequencing did not find important differences between the fall and spring cases, something that whole genome sequencing may have allowed. PAHO was unpersuaded that these were separate introduction events, and as a result Canada lost its measles elimination status in November 2025. The U.S. and Mexico may be next. Advertisement America has been trading measles cases with Canada and Mexico during the recent outbreak. Given the ongoing transmission among the countries, it would appear that measles again became endemic in North America far before it was declared as such. The extensive and ongoing sharing of cases across borders in North America raises an interesting question of whether the definition of measles chains should end at the borders of closely linked countries. As my colleagues and I review the 2026 measles elimination report, we will marvel at the extraordinary efforts of local, regional, and federal public health to rein in these outbreaks. We will be frankly amazed at the extraordinary level of detail generated by the CDC and other public health agencies. The report will reflect cutting-edge science at the top of its game. In the end, the question that will be on our minds is: Has the U.S. lost its elimination status? Some clues appear in the U.S.’s public data. The country has had measles cases every week since January 2025. That would be 20 months of weekly measles cases at this point. Of course, maybe genomic data points will save the day. Maybe they won’t. Regardless of the outcome of our meeting and PAHO’s decision in November, the U.S. clearly has a measles problem, as do Canada and Mexico. Elimination status marks whether a country has stopped sustained domestic transmission. Losing that status would signal a consequential reversal, evidence that the systems and protections that once kept measles at bay no longer do. We can and must do more than we’re doing to end the extraordinary spread of measles in our country and beyond. The nation needs strong, positive communications to the public about the importance of measles, mumps, and rubella vaccination and promotion of health care providers as trusted advisers on immunizations. A dedicated communication campaign led by the federal government would be usual in past years at this stage. These communications need clear, vocal, and regular support from the highest levels of the U.S. government. Initiatives to better understand and address vaccine hesitancy are also urgently needed. Advertisement Measles is a brutal disease that has no medical cure. Parents of children made sickest must wait it out in the hospital, despairingly wondering whether their child will die. That level of risk is indefensible in 2026 — more than 25 years after the United States achieved measles elimination. Kids are suffering and dying from a disease we already know how to prevent, and that failure is entirely ours. Noel Brewer is the Gillings distinguished professor in public health at the University of North Carolina. He chairs the U.S. committee that reviews the annual CDC measles reports to verify whether the U.S. maintains or loses measles elimination status. Letter to the editor Have an opinion on this essay? is the location, with measles cases reported in most states, including South Carolina, Utah, and Texas. The affected population includes individuals exposed to measles and those who have contracted the virus.

The event is a review process by the U.S. National Verification Committee for the Elimination of Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome.
Scale, impact and risk
As of mid-August 2026, the U.S. has reported 2,371 measles cases this year, surpassing all of 2025. There have been 34 new outbreaks this year, with Utah’s outbreak lasting over a year. The risk is that the U.S. may lose its measles elimination status due to ongoing transmission and the potential for the virus to become endemic again.
Response, status and uncertainty
National Verification Committee is reviewing the 2026 measles elimination report, and the final decision will be made by the Regional Verification Commission of PAHO in November 2026. The report includes whole genome sequencing data, which may provide more clarity. The outcome is uncertain, and confidentiality agreements prevent sharing the report’s contents.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases surge across the country. Is Oklahoma affected? - The Oklahoman

News Brief
A nationwide surge in measles cases has pushed the U.S. total to 2,318 confirmed infections in 2026—more than all of 2025 and the highest tally in 35 years, according to CDC data. Oklahoma, however, has reported only one measles case so far this year, identified in Norman during late January with no subsequent exposures. Nearly all U.S. cases this year have occurred in children aged 5–19 and among the unvaccinated, underscoring the role of low immunization rates. Measles remains highly contagious, spreading via airborne droplets and contaminated surfaces, with infected individuals infectious from four days before rash onset through four days after. Oklahoma’s MMR vaccination coverage stands at 88.7%, below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity, though two-dose MMR remains mandatory for school entry with exemptions allowed.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Life Business Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTHCARE Measles cases surge across the country. Is Oklahoma affected? Isa Almeida The Oklahoman Aug. 6, 2026, 5:04 a.m. CT The number of measles cases confirmed so far this year in the United States has reached 2,318, more than all of 2025 and the highest in 35 years, according to the latest data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. In Oklahoma, however, only one case of measles was reported in 2026. The case was reported in Norman during the week of January 26. There has been no reported exposure recently. Southern neighbor Texas has reported 190 cases in 2026, and both Utah and South Carolina have reported over 500. Despite being declared officially eliminated from the United States in 2000, measles still affects some Americans every year. The viral disease, which affected hundreds of thousands before the vaccine was licensed in 1963, had claimed under 1,000 victims annually in the 21st century, with some years having under 100 cases. That hasn't been the case in the past two years, which have seen cases rising past the 2,000 mark. Still, measles rarely results in hospitalization or deaths. The disease being eliminated from the country means there is no measles spreading within the country and new cases are only found when someone contracts measles abroad and returns to the country, but recent outbreaks have increased the numbers. According to CDC data, nearly half of measles victims in 2026 have been between the age ranges of five and 19, with 93% being unvaccinated. In Oklahoma, only 88.7% of the population has received the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine. The CDC said that when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated, most people are protected through community immunity, or herd immunity. The MMR vaccine is among the required immunizations to attend school in Oklahoma starting at kindergarten, though families can file for exemptions due to personal, religious or medical reasons. Here's what to know about measles, including symptoms and how to report it. What are measles symptoms? According to the Oklahoma State Department of Health, measles symptoms include high fever, cough, runny nose, watery eyes and a red blotchy rash starting on the face then spreading to the rest of the body. Symptoms are likely to appear as early as seven days after infection, but could be as late as 21 days. After three to five days, a red rash appears, beginning at the hairline and spreading down the rest of the body over the next few days. When the rash appears, a person’s fever may spike to more than 104 degrees, the OSDH said. Measles could affect all groups, but are particularly concerning among children under five, pregnant women, adults older than 20 years old and those with compromised immune systems. Common complications are ear infections and diarrhea. Serious complications include pneumonia, encephalitis (swelling of the brain) and in some cases, death. How is measles spread? Is measles contagious? Measles is highly contagious. According to the CDC, if one person contracts the disease up to 90% of unprotected people close to them will become infected. The virus can be passed from person to person by airborne droplets from coughing and sneezing, by contact with nose and throat secretions from ill persons or by touching a contaminated surface. Infected individuals can be contagious four days before their rash starts and through four days after the rash appears. The measles virus can live up to two hours in the air after an infected person leaves an area. If infected, the sick person must work with the health department to determine when it is safe to return to work, school, daycare or other public settings. How to prevent measles The best way to prevent measles is through the MMR vaccine, a combination of measles, mumps and rubella. The vaccine works with two doses, one recommended for all children at 12 to 15 months of age and again at four to six years of age. If someone has not received the second dose by age six, it can be taken at any point thereafter. The two doses commonly provide lifelong immunity. How to report measles in Oklahoma All Oklahoma health care providers and laboratories are mandated to report cases of disease and conditions to the OSDH, per Title 63 Oklahoma Statute (O.S.) 1981 §1-503 Measles is typically diagnosed through a combination of physical signs, health history and laboratory tests, including identifying fever with a spreading rash, throat swabs and blood tests. What vaccines are required to attend Oklahoma schools? These requirements apply to both public and private institutions, though private schools could set stricter rules or choose not to accept state-allowed exemptions. Child care (birth to three years) Four doses of DTaP (diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis) One to four doses of PCV (pneumococcal) One to four doses of Hib (Haemophilus influenza type B) One dose of varicella Three doses of IPV (polio) Two doses of Hep A (hepatitis A) Three doses of Hep B (hepatitis B) Recommended: seasonal influenza Preschool (four years) Four doses of DTaP (diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis) One dose of varicella Three doses of IPV (polio) Two doses of Hep A (hepatitis A) Three doses of Hep B (hepatitis B) Recommended: seasonal influenza, second varicella dose at four years old, IPV on or after fourth birthday Kindergarten to sixth grade Five doses of DTaP (diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis) Two doses of MMR (measles, mumps, rubella) One dose of varicella Four doses of IPV (polio) Two doses of Hep A (hepatitis A) Three doses of Hep B (hepatitis B) Recommended: seasonal influenza, second varicella dose at four years old, IPV on or after fourth birthday Seventh to 12th grade One dose of Tdap (tetanus, diphtheria, pertussis) Five doses of DTaP (diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis) Two doses of MMR (measles, mumps, rubella) One dose of varicella Four doses of IPV (polio) Two doses of Hep A (hepatitis A) Three doses of Hep B (hepatitis B) Recommended: seasonal influenza, two to three doses of HPV (human papillomavirus), one to two doses of MCV4 (meningococcal ACWY), two to three doses of Men B (meningitis group B) Post-secondary institutions These apply to all new or incoming students attending public or private post-secondary institutions in Oklahoma. Non-medical exemptions can be provided. Hepatitis B MMR (measles, mumps, rubella) Meningococcal (for students living on-campus) Who is exempt from required vaccinations in Oklahoma? In Oklahoma, there are several ways to claim an exemption from the required vaccines. Children with specific medical contraindications to any or all immunizations may be allowed to attend if the medical reason is stated and this statement is signed by a licensed physician and submitted to the school, child care facility or Head Start, according to the Oklahoma State Department of Health. Children whose parents object to immunizations based on religious teachings or personal beliefs may also seek an exemption. Isa Almeida is a trending reporter for The Oklahoman. Measles panic at popular SoCal theme park after infected guest roamed for hours - New York Post

News Brief
A measles-infected guest spent 11 hours at Universal Studios Hollywood on August 3, exposing thousands of visitors to the highly contagious virus. Los Angeles County health officials issued a public warning on August 5, urging anyone present between 10 a.m. and 9 p.m. to monitor for symptoms through August 24. Authorities emphasized the risk to unvaccinated individuals, pregnant people, infants under 12 months, and immunocompromised visitors, advising them to contact healthcare providers. Measles can spread via airborne particles and contaminated surfaces, with infected individuals contagious for up to four days before and after rash onset. The alert follows a record 2,371 U.S. measles cases in 2026, the highest since 1991, underscoring ongoing resurgence risks amid summer travel.

Location and population
Universal Studios Hollywood, Los Angeles County, California, USA; affected population includes theme park visitors during the exposure window, with emphasis on pregnant people, infants under 12 months, immunocompromised individuals, and unvaccinated persons.
Event
A measles-infected guest spent 11 hours at a crowded theme park, prompting a public health warning for potential exposure. The event is reported by the New York Post and remains under investigation by local health authorities.
Scale, impact and risk
No confirmed cases linked to this exposure reported in the evidence; risk is elevated due to measles' high contagiousness and prolonged shedding. Measles cases in the U.S. reached 2,371 in 2026 as of late July, the highest annual total since 1991. Severe complications include pneumonia, encephalitis, hospitalization, and death, particularly in unvaccinated children, pregnant individuals, and immunocompromised persons.
Response, status and uncertainty
Los Angeles County public health officials issued a warning on 2026-08-05 urging exposed individuals to monitor symptoms through 2026-08-24 and contact healthcare providers if unvaccinated or immunocompromised. Officials recommend confirming MMR vaccination status and avoiding public gatherings if symptomatic. The investigation is ongoing; no additional cases have been confirmed in relation to this exposure.

文图拉县报告九年来首例 麻疹 病例 - Ventura County Star

Ventura County reports first measles case in nine years - Ventura County Star
新闻简报加利福尼亚州文图拉县于 2026 年 8 月 5 日报告了九年来的首例 麻疹 病例,涉及一名最近从国际旅行归来的成年居民。卫生官员确认了该病例,并确定了多个暴露地点,包括奥克斯纳德的一家 Subway 餐厅、好莱坞环球影城以及奥克斯纳德和文图拉的医疗设施,暴露日期为 7 月 26 日至 8 月 1 日。患者在家中隔离。公共卫生团队正在积极追踪接触者并评估 疫苗接种 状况,同时敦促接触者(特别是未接种疫苗的人、12 个月以下的婴儿、孕妇和免疫功能低下的人)联系医生。官员们强调,该县强大的 疫苗接种 率和快速反应能力是人们对控制进一步传播充满信心的理由。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-05;未明确报告事件日期;曝光期报告为2026年7月26日至8月1日。
地点与对象
美国加利福尼亚州文图拉县;受影响的人群包括出国旅行的成年居民;暴露地点包括奥克斯纳德、文图拉和加利福尼亚州环球影城。
事件
公共卫生官员于 2026 年 8 月 5 日报告了文图拉县确诊的 麻疹 病例,这是该县九年来首例此类病例。该病例与国际旅行有关,目前处于隔离状态。
规模、影响与风险
确诊病例1例;没有死亡报告;奥克斯纳德、文图拉和环球城发现多个暴露地点;对未接种疫苗的个人、12 个月以下的婴儿、孕妇和免疫功能低下的个人的风险;麻疹 具有高度传染性。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生官员正在识别并联系接触者;测试和 疫苗接种 状态检查正在进行中;卫生官员表达了对遏制疫情的信心;麻疹 信息热线和举报热线已开通;对其他暴露地点的调查正在进行中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ventura County reports first measles case in nine years - Ventura County Star

News Brief
Ventura County, California, reported its first measles case in nine years on August 5, 2026, involving an adult resident who had recently returned from international travel. Health officials confirmed the case and identified multiple exposure sites, including a Subway restaurant in Oxnard, Universal Studios Hollywood, and medical facilities in Oxnard and Ventura, with exposure dates ranging from July 26 to August 1. The patient is isolated at home. Public health teams are actively tracing contacts and assessing vaccination status, while urging exposed individuals—particularly unvaccinated persons, infants under 12 months, pregnant individuals, and immunocompromised persons—to contact their doctors. Officials emphasized the county’s strong vaccination rates and rapid response capabilities as reasons for confidence in controlling further spread.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Opinion Entertainment Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTH Ventura County reports first measles case in nine years Tom Kisken Ventura County Star Aug. 5, 2026, 4:48 p.m. PT Ventura County's first measles cases in nine years was announced Aug. 5. Sites where other people could have been exposed include Universal Studios Hollywood. A Ventura County resident has tested positive for measles in the county’s first such case in nine years, public health officials said. Dr. Uldine Castel, county health officer, said the case is linked to international travel. She said it's possible other people could have been exposed to the highly contagious illness at several sites ranging from an Oxnard movie theater to a Ventura medical office. Los Angeles County officials said an infected person also visited Universal Studios Hollywood on July 26. Castel confirmed it was the same case. The disease was confirmed late in the afternoon on Aug. 4. Castel offered little information about the infected person but said the individual is an adult who had returned home from a trip to another country and was isolating at home. Health officials were working at identifying people who were in physical contact with the infected person and may have been exposed, Castel said. “We have found a few contacts, and they’ve all been actively engaged with our communicable disease staff,” she said, noting that in some cases blood has been drawn to confirm a person has been infected and is immune. She also expressed confidence in efforts to control further transmission. "Our team has mobilized quickly to help alert the community and try to contain the spread," she said in a news release. "Ventura County is well positioned to respond effectively with our experienced staff and strong vaccination rates among our residents." Health officials said people could possibly have been exposed at the following times and locations: July 26, about 8 a.m. to 10 a.m.: Subway restaurant, 1941 N. Rose Ave., Suite 600, Oxnard July 26, 10 a.m. to 9 p.m.: Universal Studios Hollywood, Universal City July 27 to Aug. 1: Hacienda Furniture and Mattress, 3200 S. Saviers Road, Oxnard July 31, about 7:30 p.m. to midnight.: Cinemark Century Riverpark theater, 2766 Seaglass Way, Oxnard Aug. 1, about 9:30 a.m. to 11 a.m.: Walgreens, 1801 N. Rose Ave., Oxnard Aug. 2, about 9 a.m. to 11 a.m.: Kaiser Permanente medical office, 4949 Market St., Ventura Officials said other locations of possible exposures were being investigated. They said people who may have been exposed should contact their doctors and check their vaccination status. Children should receive their first vaccine dose between 12 months and 15 months of age. The second dose should come when they are 4 years to 6 years old. Adults who may have been exposed and don't know if they've been vaccinated should reach out to their physicians. People who may have been exposed are at higher risk if they are: pregnant, an infant younger than 12 months old, a person with weakened immunities or someone who is unvaccinated. Symptoms of measles usually appear one or two weeks after exposure and can last up to 10 days. They include a high fever, cough, runny nose and red watery eyes. A rash typically appears three to five days after the first symptoms, starting at the hairline and spreading downward across the body. Public health officials said people who develop symptoms should call a health care provider immediately but should not visit a medical facility without notification. People with symptoms should isolate and stay away from work, school or other public places. Ventura County’s last reported measles cases came in 2017 when three county residents and two other non-residents staying in the county tested positive for the illness. Two years earlier, when an outbreak linked to Disneyland spread across California and other states, 14 measles cases were reported in the county. This year has brought a surge of measles cases in the United States. Castel said the local case is not linked to other domestic outbreaks because it was triggered by international travel. Castel said the county’s high measles vaccination rate and the quick response of health workers are good signs. “I feel really confident,” she said. "We have an incredible staff that knows how to do this." People with questions can call the county’s measles information line at 805-652-7599. Doctors can report suspected cases by calling 805-981-5201. Tom Kisken covers health care and other news for the Ventura County Star. Reach him at tom.kisken@vcstar.com. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

研究发现单身美国麻疹 暴发可能花费数百万美元:如何保护自己

Study finds single US measles outbreak can cost millions: How to protect yourself
新闻简报A study published on August 5, 2026, by the New Mexico Department of Health and the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) revealed the financial impact of a measles outbreak in the United States in 2025. The outbreak originated from an undervaccinated community in Texas and spread to New Mexico, affecting 100 confirmed cases, including 57 adults and 43 children. The total cost of the outbreak was estimated at $5.4 million per patient, with $3.2 million allocated to public health response activities such as vaccination campaigns and contact tracing. Direct medical costs totaled $290,000, and productivity losses amounted to $1.6 million. The study highlighted that the true cost was likely higher due to unaccounted expenses like over-the-counter medications and indirect costs. Researchers stressed that prevention through vaccination is the most effective and cost-efficient strategy against measles, especially with declining vaccination rates in the country.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究发表于2026年8月5日,暴发发生于2025年。
地点与对象
暴发 从德克萨斯州一个疫苗接种不足的社区传播到新墨西哥州,影响了 100 例确诊病例,涉及 57 名成人和 43 名儿童。
事件
新墨西哥州卫生部和美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 牵头的一项新研究揭示了 2025 年美国 麻疹 暴发 从德克萨斯州疫苗接种不足的社区传播到新墨西哥州的财务成本。
规模、影响与风险
The outbreak resulted in a total cost of $5.4 million per patient, with $3.2 million allocated to public health response activities, $290,000 in direct medical costs, and $1.6 million in productivity losses. The study also noted that the true cost was likely higher due to unaccounted expenses.
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究包括医疗费用、疫苗接种 计划、公共卫生应对、检疫和生产力损失。研究人员强调,预防是针对 麻疹 的最佳且最便宜的防御措施,而 暴发 则强调了保持高 疫苗接种 率的重要性。

Study finds single US measles outbreak can cost millions: How to protect yourself

News Brief
A study published on August 5, 2026, by the New Mexico Department of Health and the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) revealed the financial impact of a measles outbreak in the United States in 2025. The outbreak originated from an undervaccinated community in Texas and spread to New Mexico, affecting 100 confirmed cases, including 57 adults and 43 children. The total cost of the outbreak was estimated at $5.4 million per patient, with $3.2 million allocated to public health response activities such as vaccination campaigns and contact tracing. Direct medical costs totaled $290,000, and productivity losses amounted to $1.6 million. The study highlighted that the true cost was likely higher due to unaccounted expenses like over-the-counter medications and indirect costs. Researchers stressed that prevention through vaccination is the most effective and cost-efficient strategy against measles, especially with declining vaccination rates in the country.

Learn practical prevention tips and why vaccination remains the strongest defense. Written By: N. Lothungbeni Humtsoe | Published : August 5, 2026 10:15 AM IST Share Share Article ✕ Copied Measles in US. Measles is a highly contagious illness that spreads from person to person through the air and via respiratory droplets. According to the World Health Organization (WHO) about 95,000 people died in connection with the illness in 2024. Although vaccination has decreased the death rate of measles from 750,000 in 2000 to 95,000 in 2025, scientists state that a single measles outbreak in the country can cost billions of dollars. A study published in the Annals of Internal Medicine indicates that nearly $5.4 million was invested in preventing the US measles outbreak in 2025. This figure includes expenses for medical care and public health services for vaccination-related activities of patients affected by the contagious illness. The outbreak resulted in quarantine, emergency vaccination campaigns, hospitalisations and widespread public health measures beyond the 100 confirmed cases indicating the actual burden of measles is far greater than the patient. Researchers say each case of measles triggers a chain reaction, affecting families, health care providers and public health systems. With declining vaccination rates in the United States, experts say taking measures to prevent outbreaks is much cheaper and more effective than once the virus spreads. Also Read US issues chikungunya travel advisory: Countries affected, symptoms and tips to stay safe 'Explosive diarrhoea' outbreak spread across US: What you need to know before eating fresh produce Parasite causing 'explosive diarrhoea' is sickening Americans: Dos and don'ts Highest number of measles cases in 35 years The study led by researchers from the New Mexico Department of Health (NMDOH) and the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) looked at the financial costs of measles spreading from an undervaccinated community in Texas to New Mexico in 2025. Costs of health services, vaccination programs, public health response, quarantine and productivity losses were included in the study. Measles cases included 100 confirmed cases involving 57 adults and 43 children. Additionally 205 exposed contacts were quarantined and 133 public health workers were engaged in controlling transmission. The total cost of the study was estimated at almost $5.4 million per patient or an excess of $53,000 per patient. The highest cost was for the public health response at $3.2 million of which $2.1 million was spent on vaccination response activities including contact tracing, emergency immunisation and surveillance. The direct medical costs from the outbreak totalled $290,000 with $238,000 spent on hospitalisation and $32,000 on outpatient visits and $20,000 on 1286 laboratory tests. You may like to read Outbreaks also negatively affected the economy of families where nearly $1.6 million was lost in productivity from missed work among adult patients, caregivers, quarantined contacts and one who died. However the researchers noted that the true cost was probably even higher leaving out the costs of over-the-counter medications, travel for medical treatment and indirect costs. The results come after the US reported over 2,300 measles cases this year which is the largest number of cases recorded in any single year in 35 years due to increasing concern about low vaccination rates in the country. Tips to prevent measles outbreak The findings of the study are clear that measles is preventable but the consequences of not vaccinating can be a burden for health services and society. It is important to note that prevention is the best and cheapest defence against measles. These include: Keep up to date with the MMR vaccine which provides the best protection Keep children up-to-date with all recommended vaccinations Stay out of contact with people who are sick with signs like hay fever, cough, red eyes or rash Handwashing and good respiratory hygiene are non-negotiable Seek immediate medical treatment if you develop symptoms Confine infected people immediately to prevent transmission of the virus Disclaimer: This article is for informational purposes only and not medical advice. 可预防的死亡,可预防的失败:向 Ruma 的 麻疹 暴发 学习

Preventable deaths, preventable failures: Learning from Ruma’s measles outbreak
新闻简报自六月初以来,孟加拉国班多尔班山区鲁马乌帕齐拉的一名 麻疹 暴发 已杀死至少 7 名原住民儿童,另有数十人住院。这场悲剧暴露了深刻的系统性差距:常规免疫覆盖率低、因文化和地理障碍而导致的就医延迟、疾病弱监测以及偏远山区和森林社区的紧急转诊途径不足。季风降雨和山体滑坡进一步孤立了村庄,延迟了获得护理的时间,并导致未接种疫苗的儿童迅速传播。专家强调,这场危机反映了医疗服务方面更广泛的不平等,而不仅仅是 疫苗 的失败,他们敦促采取文化响应型外展 疫苗接种、基于社区的 监测、适合地形的救护车以及对当地卫生人力的投资,以防止未来出现 疫苗。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Preventable deaths, preventable failures: Learning from Ruma’s measles outbreak

News Brief
A measles outbreak in Ruma Upazila, Bandarban Hill District, Bangladesh, has killed at least seven Indigenous children since early June, with dozens more hospitalized. The tragedy exposes deep systemic gaps: low routine immunization coverage, delayed care-seeking rooted in cultural and geographic barriers, weak disease surveillance, and inadequate emergency referral pathways in remote hill and forest communities. Monsoon rains and landslides further isolate villages, delaying access to care and enabling rapid spread among unvaccinated children. Experts emphasize that the crisis reflects broader inequities in health access rather than vaccine failure alone, urging culturally responsive outreach vaccination, community-based surveillance, terrain-appropriate ambulances, and investment in the local health workforce to prevent future outbreaks.
Original source text (English)

Ad More Sports TBS Graduates Bangladesh Supplement Infograph Explainer Archive Gallery Long Read Interviews Offbeat Magazine Climate Change Health Cartoons বাংলা SATURDAY, AUGUST 08, 2026 Preventable deaths, preventable failures: Learning from Ruma’s measles outbreak Thoughts Sumit Banik 04 August, 2026, 06:15 pm Last modified: 04 August, 2026, 06:28 pm Preventable deaths, preventable failures: Learning from Ruma’s measles outbreak The measles outbreak in Bandarban’s remote Ruma upazila is more than a local health emergency. The deaths of at least seven Indigenous children have exposed how gaps in immunisation, surveillance, emergency referral systems, and culturally responsive healthcare continue to leave some of Bangladesh’s most isolated communities dangerously vulnerable Sumit Banik 04 August, 2026, 06:15 pm Last modified: 04 August, 2026, 06:28 pm Photo Caption: Khumi parents care for their child in the isolation ward at Ruma Upazila Health Complex amid a measles outbreak that has killed seven children in Bandarban's remote Ruma upazila. Photo: TBS The recent measles outbreak in Ruma Upazila of Bangladesh's Bandarban Hill District has exposed a painful reality: for some children, where they are born can determine whether they live or die. Since early June, dozens of children from remote Indigenous communities have fallen ill with measles. According to media reports, "Seven Khumi children die as Measles outbreak strikes Bandarban's Ruma" ultimately at least seven children have died, while many others have required medical treatment. Behind these heartbreaking statistics lies a deeper public health story—one that extends far beyond a single infectious disease outbreak. Measles is not a mysterious or untreatable disease. It is one of the world's most contagious viral infections, yet it is also one of the most preventable through routine immunisation. When children die from measles today, the tragedy rarely reflects the virus alone. More often, it reveals gaps in immunisation coverage, delayed care-seeking, weak disease surveillance, inadequate referral systems, and persistent inequities in access to healthcare. The situation in Ruma illustrates exactly this reality. Many of the affected children belong to the Mro and Khumi Indigenous communities living in some of Bangladesh's most geographically isolated areas. Their villages are scattered across steep hills, dense forests, and locations that often require hours of walking before reaching the nearest road. During the monsoon season, travel becomes even more difficult as heavy rains, landslides, and swollen streams further isolate communities. Keep updated, follow The Business Standard's Google news channel Geography, however, is only part of the challenge. Health-seeking behaviour in many Indigenous communities has evolved over generations within unique cultural, social, and spiritual traditions. Families may initially seek advice from community elders or traditional healers before considering formal healthcare. Language barriers, financial hardship, limited health literacy, and previous experiences with inaccessible services may further delay treatment. These realities should never be interpreted as resistance to modern medicine. Rather, they highlight the importance of designing health systems that are culturally responsive, respectful, and trusted by the communities they serve. From an epidemiological perspective, the outbreak demonstrates how infectious diseases exploit health system vulnerabilities. Measles spreads rapidly wherever immunity gaps exist. A single infected child can transmit the virus to many susceptible children, particularly in communities with low vaccination coverage. Once transmission begins in remote areas where early detection is difficult and referral pathways are weak, controlling the outbreak becomes significantly more challenging. This is why outbreak preparedness cannot begin after children start dying. Public health systems must identify vulnerable populations before outbreaks occur. Regular vaccination coverage assessments, active surveillance, rapid response teams, and community engagement should become routine components of healthcare delivery in hard-to-reach areas rather than emergency measures implemented only after a crisis emerges. The Ruma outbreak also reminds us that equity is one of the strongest determinants of health. Although Bangladesh has achieved remarkable success in childhood immunisation over the past decades, national averages can conceal local disparities. A country may report high vaccination coverage while small pockets of under-immunised populations remain vulnerable. These "zero-dose" or under-vaccinated communities often become the epicentre of outbreaks. Reaching the last child is invariably the most difficult—but it is also the most important. A comprehensive response requires more than emergency vaccination campaigns. First, immunisation services must be redesigned for difficult terrain. Instead of expecting families to travel long distances to health facilities, outreach vaccination teams should routinely visit remote villages with reliable cold-chain support and adequate staffing. Second, community-based surveillance should be strengthened. Local volunteers, teachers, religious leaders, and trained community health workers can help identify suspected cases early and notify health authorities before widespread transmission occurs. Third, emergency referral systems require urgent investment. In many parts of the Chittagong Hill Tracts, reaching a hospital can take several hours or even an entire day. Dedicated ambulances suitable for mountainous terrain, community transport mechanisms, emergency communication networks, and referral coordination can significantly reduce delays in life-saving treatment. Fourth, culturally appropriate risk communication is essential. Health education materials should be available in local languages and developed in partnership with Indigenous leaders, ensuring that vaccination campaigns are built on trust rather than assumptions. Communities are far more likely to participate when health interventions acknowledge their culture and involve respected local voices. Fifth, the health workforce serving remote hill districts deserves greater investment. Recruiting and retaining trained healthcare professionals in geographically isolated regions remains a persistent challenge. Incentive packages, continuous professional support, telemedicine consultations, and training local community members as frontline health workers can help bridge this gap. The lessons extend beyond Bandarban. Climate change, extreme weather events, and difficult terrain will continue to complicate healthcare delivery in many remote parts of Bangladesh. Future outbreaks—whether measles, dengue, cholera, or emerging infectious diseases—will disproportionately affect communities that remain underserved by the health system. Strengthening resilience in these areas should therefore be viewed not merely as a humanitarian obligation but as a national public health priority. Ultimately, this outbreak is not simply about measles. It is about whether every child in Bangladesh enjoys an equal opportunity to survive, regardless of ethnicity, geography, or socioeconomic status. It is about whether health equity remains an aspiration or becomes an operational principle guiding public policy. The deaths of these children should not be remembered only as another headline from a remote hill district. They should become a turning point for strengthening inclusive healthcare, improving outbreak preparedness, and ensuring that no child dies from a vaccine-preventable disease simply because healthcare arrives too late. Distance should never determine survival. Every child deserves timely vaccination, prompt diagnosis, quality treatment, and an equal chance at life. The measles outbreak in Ruma is a painful reminder of how far we still have to go—but it also offers a clear roadmap for building a stronger, more equitable, and more resilient public health system for all Bangladeshis. Sumit Banik is a Public Health Activist and Trainer. Maryland confirms new measles case after Elkton exposure - WBAL-TV

News Brief
Maryland health officials confirmed the state’s 13th measles case of 2026 after a possible exposure at ChristianaCare Union Hospital’s emergency department in Elkton on August 3. The Maryland Department of Health warned that anyone who visited the emergency department between 3:00 a.m. and 11:00 a.m. may have been exposed, noting measles can spread through airborne particles or contaminated surfaces. Symptoms typically appear 7–21 days after exposure, and exposed individuals are urged to monitor for fever, cough, runny nose, and red eyes for 21 days. Health authorities recommend vaccination for unprotected individuals and advise contacting a provider if symptoms develop, with post-exposure treatments available for certain exposures. The investigation remains active as officials track potential secondary cases.

Advertisement Updated: 4:24 PM EDT Aug 4, 2026 Editorial Standards i Julian Nazario Digital Content Producer Maryland confirms 13th measles case after possible exposure at Elkton hospital Updated: 4:24 PM EDT Aug 4, 2026 Editorial Standards i Julian Nazario Digital Content Producer The Maryland Department of Health said it had confirmed a case of measles in a Maryland resident after a possible exposure in the Upper Eastern Shore region. The new case is the 13th confirmed case in the state this year. Advertisement According to MDH, anyone who visited ChristianaCare Union Hospital Emergency Department in Elkton on Aug. 2 from 7 p.m. to 10:15 p.m. may have been exposed to the infection. Last week, the department had warned of a possible exposure at the same emergency room on July 30, warning those who visited the department from 3:00 a.m. - 7:15 a.m. It can take up to three weeks for symptoms to appear. Think you may have been exposed? Here's what to do Were you vaccinated for measles? If you have received two doses of a measles-containing vaccine, or were born before 1957, you are generally considered protected. Check your immunization status securely online via My Immunization Record (MyIR). MDH recommends all eligible people get fully vaccinated against measles. The measles vaccine is routinely recommended at 12-15 months of age for the first dose and the second dose at 4-6 years old. Sometimes a dose is given as early as six to 12 months if there is travel planned to an area that has ongoing measles transmission. Monitor for early symptoms : People, especially those not fully vaccinated or otherwise immune to measles, who were at any of these locations during the possible exposure times should monitor themselves for any early symptoms of measles for 21 days after the potential exposure. Sorry, this video is not available, please check back later. Video above: Doctor shares tips amid confirmed measles case in Maryland Not fully vaccinated or otherwise immune? If you are not fully vaccinated or otherwise immune to measles (i.e., you already had measles) and you might have been exposed, call your health care provider or your local health department to discuss the best next steps for you. If your exposure occurred on or after April 14, 2026, you may qualify for post-exposure treatments. What are the symptoms of measles? Early symptoms of measles are a fever of more than 101 degrees Fahrenheit; runny nose; cough; and red, watery eyes. Usually, one to four days after the early symptoms, a red rash appears on the face and spreads to the rest of the body. Symptoms typically develop 10 to 14 days after exposure but can develop as soon as seven days and as long as 21 days after exposure. A person with measles is contagious, beginning four days before the rash appears until four days after the rash begins. Call your health care provider : Exposed individuals who develop a fever or other symptoms of measles should not go to child care, school, work, or out in public, and should contact their health care provider. They should call their health care provider before going to a waiting room or emergency department so that the facility can take measures to prevent measles from spreading to others. Is measles contagious? Health officials describe measles as a highly contagious disease that is spread easily through the air when an infectious person breathes, coughs or sneezes. The virus can remain in the air for up to two hours after the infected person leaves the area. The disease is also spread by direct contact and after touching infected surfaces. Clackamas Kaiser Clinic Becomes Latest Measles Site as Oregon Cases Climb
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 7 日,俄勒冈州卫生局在一名受感染患者上周四就诊后确认,位于克拉克马斯县的 Kaiser Permanente Mt. Scott 医疗办公室是 麻疹 暴露地点。感染者离开后,该病毒可以在空气中保持传染性长达两个小时,从而增加了封闭空间中 传播 的风险。这是波特兰都会区一系列暴露警报中的最新一次,包括之前在其他 Kaiser Permanente 设施和非 Kaiser 场所发生的事件。俄勒冈州今年报告了 40 起 麻疹 病例,疫苗接种 比率下降导致了疫情蔓延。俄勒冈州卫生局建议 疫苗接种 预防感染,并建议接触者在前往医疗机构之前提前致电,以避免将病毒传播给其他人。他们还实施了废水 监测 系统来监测污水中的 麻疹病毒 浓度,有助于及早发现 麻疹病毒。该机构强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,并指出两剂 MMR 疫苗 预防 麻疹 的有效率为 97%。
查看新闻五要素
时间
上周四下午,一名受感染的患者前往 Clackamas Kaiser 诊所就诊后,俄勒冈州卫生局于 2026 年 8 月 7 日发布了暴露警报。正如疾病预防控制中心的指南所述,感染者离开后,该病毒可以在空气中保持传染性长达两个小时。
地点与对象
该事件发生在俄勒冈州克拉克马斯县的凯撒医疗机构斯科特山医疗办公室。受影响的人群包括未接种疫苗的居民和在诊所就诊期间可能接触过的个人。
事件
俄勒冈州卫生局确认 Clackamas Kaiser 诊所是 麻疹 暴露地点。这是遵循波特兰都市区的暴露警报模式,包括之前在其他 Kaiser Permanente 设施和非 Kaiser 场所发生的事件。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 8 月 7 日,俄勒冈州今年已记录 40 起 麻疹 病例。俄勒冈州卫生局已将该诊所标记为最新确认的暴露地点,强调了 传播 在封闭空间中的风险以及 疫苗接种 对防止进一步传播的重要性。
应对、状态与不确定性
俄勒冈州卫生局敦促有 麻疹 症状的个人在前往医疗机构之前提前致电。他们还推出了全州范围的废水 麻疹病毒 仪表板来跟踪污水中的 麻疹病毒 浓度,即使不进行临床测试,也有助于识别潜在的传播。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Clackamas Kaiser Clinic Becomes Latest Measles Site as Oregon Cases Climb

News Brief
On August 7, 2026, the Oregon Health Authority confirmed that the Kaiser Permanente Mt. Scott Medical Office in Clackamas County was a measles exposure site, following an infected patient's visit last Thursday. The virus can remain infectious in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves, increasing the risk of transmission in enclosed spaces. This marks the latest in a series of exposure alerts in the Portland metro area, including previous incidents at other Kaiser Permanente facilities and non-Kaiser sites. Oregon has reported 40 measles cases this year, with falling vaccination rates contributing to the spread. The Oregon Health Authority recommends vaccination to prevent infection and advises those exposed to call ahead before visiting medical facilities to avoid spreading the virus to others. They have also implemented a wastewater surveillance system to monitor measles virus concentrations in sewage, aiding in early detection of outbreaks. The agency emphasizes the importance of vaccination, noting that two doses of the MMR vaccine are 97% effective at preventing measles.

Subscribe More forecasts: Atlanta 30 day forecast Portland Clackamas Kaiser Clinic Becomes Latest Measles Site as Oregon Cases Climb By Samuel Hansen Published on August 07, 2026 Source: Google Street View People who visited the Kaiser Permanente Mt. Scott Medical Office in Clackamas last Thursday afternoon may have been exposed to measles, according to the Oregon Health Authority. The agency says anyone at the clinic between 1:35 p.m. and 3:44 p.m. last Thursday could have encountered the virus, making it the latest site in a string of Portland-area exposure alerts this year. The clinic sits at 9800 Southeast Sunnyside Road in Clackamas County, according to KGW, which reported that state and county health officials identified the site after an infected patient spent more than two hours there. As The Oregonian/OregonLive reports, the Oregon Health Authority has flagged this Kaiser Permanente location as the latest confirmed exposure point in the Portland metro area, with other recent risk areas including Clark County, Beaverton, Gresham and parts of Portland itself. Measles spreads through coughs and sneezes, and the virus can remain infectious in the air of an enclosed space for up to two hours after an infected person leaves, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Symptoms typically appear seven to 21 days after exposure and can include fever, cough, runny nose and red eyes, per the same account, sometimes followed by a rash of flat red spots that begins on the face and spreads across the body. Oregon Health Authority guidelines note that infected individuals become contagious four days before the rash appears and remain contagious for four days after it shows up, which helps explain how the virus can spread in a waiting room before anyone realizes they are sick. A Pattern Stretching Back to January This is not the first time a Kaiser Permanente facility in Clackamas County has landed on the exposure list this year. Clackamas County confirmed its first measles case of 2026 in mid-January, which triggered a potential exposure alert at the Kaiser Permanente Sunnyside Medical Center emergency department on January 26, according to Oregon Public Broadcasting. Health officials at the time noted that most confirmed local cases involved unvaccinated residents without international travel history. Less than a month before Thursday's alert, three Providence Health facilities — Providence Immediate Care in Happy Valley, Providence Willamette Falls Medical Center in Oregon City, and Providence Portland Medical Center — were flagged as exposure sites between July 9 and July 12, Oregon Public Broadcasting reported. Urgent care centers and emergency departments have repeatedly become exposure points this year when infected patients arrive without calling ahead, a pattern Hoodline has tracked through incidents from a midnight ER scare in Oregon City to a Beaverton trampoline park scare last month. Why Oregon Keeps Seeing New Exposure Sites The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported 2,465 confirmed measles cases across 47 U.S. jurisdictions as of Thursday, making 2026 the worst year for measles nationally in more than three decades since the disease was declared eliminated in the country in 2000. The Oregonian/OregonLive reports that Oregon itself has recorded 40 measles cases this year, according to CDC figures. Falling vaccination rates help explain why the Portland area keeps generating new exposure sites. The Oregon Health Authority reported in May that nonmedical vaccine exemptions among Oregon kindergarteners hit a record 10.9% for the 2025–2026 school year, up from 6.9% just a few years earlier, with exemptions for the second MMR dose reaching 9.4%. Separately, state immunization data showed only 85.6% of Oregon kindergarteners were up to date on required vaccines that same year, leaving more than a third of the state's K-12 schools below the 93% threshold that epidemiologists say is needed for herd immunity, according to The Lund Report. To catch spread that clinical testing might miss, the Oregon Health Authority launched a statewide wastewater surveillance dashboard in February that tracks measles virus concentrations in sewage across the state's counties, since infected people shed the virus whether or not they seek medical care. Hoodline previously covered the launch of that quiet wastewater alarm system. What To Do If You Were Exposed Measles has been largely non-fatal in countries where the vaccine is available, but it can be more dangerous for children, older adults and immunocompromised people, per The Oregonian/OregonLive. Two doses of the MMR vaccine are 97% effective at preventing measles, and the Oregon Health Authority recommends vaccination to lower infection risk. The CDC notes that susceptible people exposed to measles can still receive an MMR vaccine within 72 hours of exposure to prevent or lessen illness, or immune globulin within six days for high-risk patients such as infants, pregnant women or immunocompromised individuals. The Oregon Health Authority is urging anyone experiencing measles symptoms, including a rash or runny nose, to call ahead before visiting a medical facility rather than walking into a waiting room. Calling ahead helps prevent exposing other patients who may not be immune, health officials say, a message that has repeated through nearly every exposure alert issued across the Portland metro area this year. Ventura County reports first measles case in nearly a decade - NBC Los Angeles

News Brief
Ventura County, California, reported its first measles case in nearly a decade on August 5, 2026, linked to international travel. Health officials confirmed the case and identified multiple public exposure sites in Oxnard and Ventura between July 26 and August 1, including a Subway restaurant, Hacienda Furniture and Mattress, Cinemark Century Riverpark, Walgreens, and Kaiser Permanente facilities. Measles is a highly contagious airborne disease that can cause severe complications. The county’s public health department is actively investigating, alerting the community, and working with healthcare providers to prevent further spread. Officials noted that unvaccinated or partially vaccinated individuals are at higher risk, while those fully vaccinated or born before 1957 have very low risk. Residents with potential exposure are advised to contact health authorities for guidance.
Original source text (English)

Measles outbreak a struggle for families - JHNewsAndGuide.com

News Brief
Teton County, Wyoming, has confirmed a third measles case in an ongoing outbreak, prompting county commissioners to declare a local emergency. The announcement was reported on August 5, 2026, by the Jackson Hole News and Guide. Public health authorities have not disclosed the total number of cases, exposure details, or specific control measures implemented. The declaration underscores escalating concern within the community, though further specifics about investigation, testing, or containment efforts remain unreported in the available evidence.
