New Mexico measles outbreak costs estimated at $5.4 million, including vaccination efforts - Bioengineer.org

New Mexico measles outbreak costs estimated at $5.4 million, including vaccination efforts - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报A measles outbreak in New Mexico in 2025 resulted in an estimated $5.4 million in societal costs, according to a study published in the Annals of Internal Medicine. The outbreak involved 100 confirmed cases, 205 exposed contacts, and 133 public health responders. The cost exceeded $53,000 per case, with public health activities accounting for $3.2 million and vaccination efforts representing $2.1 million. The outbreak included one death, highlighting the economic and health impacts of measles. The analysis, conducted by researchers from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and the New Mexico Department of Health, emphasized the importance of vaccination coverage and preparedness in preventing such outbreaks. The estimate reflects the combined value of labor, services, time, and resources used to control the outbreak and manage its consequences, but it may not capture all potential downstream effects.
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发发生在2025年,新闻报道发布于2026年8月3日。
地点与对象
暴发 位于新墨西哥州,涉及 100 例确诊病例、205 名暴露接触者和 133 名公共卫生响应人员。
事件
A measles outbreak in New Mexico in 2025 generated an estimated $5.4 million in societal costs, including vaccination efforts, according to a new analysis published in Annals of Internal Medicine. The event is confirmed as a measles outbreak.
规模、影响与风险
The outbreak included one death and involved a substantial network of people requiring monitoring or intervention. The cost exceeded $53,000 per case, and public health activities accounted for approximately $3.2 million, with vaccination-related work representing about $2.1 million. The risk is particularly serious for infants, pregnant people, and individuals with weakened immune systems.
应对、状态与不确定性
来自疾病控制和预防中心国家免疫和呼吸系统疾病中心以及新墨西哥州卫生部的研究人员检查了这一反应。该分析采用了社会视角,并包括了研究人员记录的成本,但估计值可能无法代表所有可能的下游影响。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

New Mexico measles outbreak costs estimated at $5.4 million, including vaccination efforts - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A measles outbreak in New Mexico in 2025 resulted in an estimated $5.4 million in societal costs, according to a study published in the Annals of Internal Medicine. The outbreak involved 100 confirmed cases, 205 exposed contacts, and 133 public health responders. The cost exceeded $53,000 per case, with public health activities accounting for $3.2 million and vaccination efforts representing $2.1 million. The outbreak included one death, highlighting the economic and health impacts of measles. The analysis, conducted by researchers from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and the New Mexico Department of Health, emphasized the importance of vaccination coverage and preparedness in preventing such outbreaks. The estimate reflects the combined value of labor, services, time, and resources used to control the outbreak and manage its consequences, but it may not capture all potential downstream effects.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, August 7, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health New Mexico measles outbreak costs estimated at $5.4 million, including vaccination efforts by Bioengineer August 3, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 4 mins read 0 Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A measles outbreak in New Mexico in 2025 generated an estimated $5.4 million in societal costs, according to a new analysis published in Annals of Internal Medicine. The estimate includes the resources used to investigate and contain the outbreak, medical care for infected people, and productivity losses among patients, families, contacts, and public health workers. Averaged across the 100 confirmed cases, the cost exceeded $53,000 per case, illustrating how quickly the economic consequences of a vaccine-preventable viral disease can extend beyond hospitals and clinics. The outbreak included one death and involved a substantial network of people who required monitoring or intervention. Researchers from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s National Center for Immunization and Respiratory Diseases and the New Mexico Department of Health examined the response involving 100 confirmed patients, 205 exposed contacts, and 133 public health responders. Their analysis used a societal perspective, a standard economic framework that attempts to capture costs borne by health systems, government agencies, individuals, employers, and the wider community rather than focusing only on direct medical expenses. Discover more healthcare infections vaccine Health Measles is caused by measles virus, a highly contagious respiratory pathogen that spreads when an infected person breathes, coughs, or sneezes. The virus can remain suspended in the air for a period of time, allowing transmission even after an infected person has left an enclosed space. Once a case is identified, health officials must move rapidly to determine where transmission may have occurred, identify susceptible people, assess vaccination histories, and monitor exposed individuals during the incubation period. Those activities can consume considerable personnel time even when they prevent additional cases. The analysis estimated that public health activities accounted for approximately $3.2 million of the outbreak’s total cost. Vaccination-related work represented about $2.1 million of that amount, making it the largest individual component of the public health response. These efforts can include locating and reviewing immunization records, organizing vaccination clinics, administering doses, communicating with affected communities, and providing post-exposure vaccination when appropriate. Although such measures require immediate investment, they are intended to interrupt transmission before more people become infected. Containing measles also requires a detailed case-investigation infrastructure. Public health teams must verify diagnoses, collect and interpret laboratory information, reconstruct patients’ movements, notify people who may have been exposed, and coordinate with schools, health-care facilities, workplaces, and community organizations. Officials may need to recommend exclusion from group settings for people who are infected or who lack evidence of immunity. Each additional case can therefore create a new chain of investigations and notifications, multiplying the workload even when the number of patients remains relatively small. The researchers also included medical costs and productivity losses in their estimate. Medical expenses can arise from outpatient visits, diagnostic testing, emergency care, hospitalization, treatment of complications, and follow-up. Measles may cause fever, cough, conjunctivitis, and a characteristic rash, but it can also lead to pneumonia, severe dehydration, inflammation of the brain, and other complications. The risk is particularly serious for infants, pregnant people, and individuals with weakened immune systems. When patients or caregivers miss work, schools close temporarily, or responders are diverted from other duties, the economic effects extend beyond direct treatment costs. The $5.4 million estimate is therefore not a price tag for clinical care alone. It reflects the combined value of labor, services, time, and resources used to control the outbreak and manage its consequences. Because the analysis focused on a defined outbreak and included the costs documented by investigators, the estimate may not represent every possible downstream effect. At the same time, it provides a concrete measure of the scale of resources required to contain measles once transmission begins in a community. Discover more Health Conditions Biological Sciences Finance public health Medical Facilities & Services The findings highlight the economic role of routine vaccination coverage. Measles-containing vaccines train the immune system to recognize the virus before exposure, reducing the number of susceptible people available to sustain transmission. When coverage is high, imported infections are less likely to produce large outbreaks because transmission encounters immune individuals more often. When immunity gaps develop, however, a single infection can trigger extensive contact tracing, emergency vaccination, public communication, and medical evaluation. Maintaining vaccination coverage can therefore function as both a clinical intervention and a form of outbreak-cost prevention. The New Mexico experience also demonstrates why preparedness is central to viral disease control. Public health agencies need surveillance systems capable of detecting suspected cases, laboratories able to confirm infection, trained personnel who can conduct rapid investigations, and clear channels for communicating with the public. The study’s authors argue that these capacities, together with strong vaccination programs, can help communities avoid the substantial health and economic consequences associated with measles. The outbreak’s estimated cost underscores a broader lesson in infectious-disease science: preventing transmission before a virus gains a foothold is often far less disruptive than responding after spread has begun. Subject of Research: People Article Title: Economic Impact of the 2025 New Mexico Measles Outbreak: Highlighting the Cost of Efforts to Prevent Future Outbreaks News Publication Date: 4-Aug-2026 Web References: https://www.acpjournals.org/doi/10.7326/ANNALS-26-03275-IM References: https://doi.org/10.7326/ANNALS-26-00718 Keywords: Measles, Vaccination, Public health Tags: cost of measles outbreak in New Mexicoimpact of measles on healthcare resourcesinfectious disease economic burdenmeasles outbreak economic impactmeasles virus transmission and containmentoutbreak investigation and contact tracing costspublic health response to measlespublic health response to viral outbreakssocietal costs of infectious disease outbreakssocietal perspective in health economicsvaccination efforts cost analysisvaccine-preventable disease costs Share12Tweet7Share2ShareShareShare1 Related Posts Eli Lilly’s Thomas Marron to Present at 13th ARDD Meeting in Boston August 7, 2026 Afghan Refugee Women in U.S. Face Health and Healthcare Access Challenges August 7, 2026 Standardized chronic disease care lowers comorbidity risk among Chinese patients August 7, 2026 Metabolic Reprogramming Converts Pancreatic Alpha Cells into Insulin-Producing Beta Cells August 7, 2026 POPULAR NEWS Deep learning improves communication between bionic eyes and the brain 29 shares Share 12 Tweet 7 Revealing How Cancer Cells Evade the Immune System 29 shares Share 12 Tweet 7 Eli Lilly’s Thomas Marron to Present at 13th ARDD Meeting in Boston 29 shares Share 12 Tweet 7 Logging practices expose hidden damage in Sal forests 29 shares Share 12 Tweet 7 Welcome Back! B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

俄亥俄州 麻疹 暴发 再次增长,2026 例病例总数与 2025 年持平

Ohio measles outbreak grows again, 2026 cases match 2025 total
新闻简报截至 2026 年 8 月,俄亥俄州正在进行的 麻疹 麻疹 病例已达到 10 个县的 45 例,与该州 2025 年的总数相当,标志着今年将出现第三波病例。卫生官员将疫情复苏归因于 麻疹 比率低于 95% 的群体免疫阈值,到 2023 年,全州 MMR 覆盖率将达到 91.5%。在全国范围内,美国 麻疹 病例已达到 35 年来的最高水平,2026 年报告了 2,295 例,超过了去年的总数。公共卫生警告强调该疾病的可预防性,以及未接种疫苗的儿童和免疫功能低下的人面临肺炎和脑炎等并发症的更高风险。俄亥俄州卫生部和当地机构正在进行接触者追踪并发布暴露警报,同时敦促在返校季节之前重新开展 疫苗接种 工作。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-06;该活动持续到 2026 年 7 月至 8 月,病例数于 2026 年 8 月更新(N13、N18、N20、N28)。
地点与对象
美国俄亥俄州;10 个县(阿什塔比拉、凯霍加、富兰克林、格奥加、霍姆斯、诺克斯、渥太华、特朗布尔、塔斯卡瓦斯、韦恩);受影响的年龄范围为 15 个月至 20 岁(N13、N20、N21)。
事件
确认俄亥俄州卫生部和当地卫生部门报告的麻疹 暴发;2026年第三波暴发;已发布公共卫生警告(N3、N4、N8、N13、N14、N15、N16)。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年俄亥俄州确诊病例数为 45 例,与 2025 年总数相当;2026 年全国病例数为 2,295 例,超过 2025 年的 2,286 例(35 年来新高);强调未接种疫苗的儿童和免疫功能低下群体的风险;麻疹 并发症包括肺炎和脑炎(N5、N8、N13、N18、N49、N50、N56)。
应对、状态与不确定性
俄亥俄州卫生部和当地卫生部门进行病例调查、接触者追踪和公众暴露通知;敦促开展 疫苗接种 活动;俄亥俄州 2023 年 MMR 覆盖率预计为 91.5%;未达到群体免疫阈值;未来传播和 疫苗接种 吸收(N7、N14、N15、N16、N34、N35、N38、N44、N46)仍存在不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio measles outbreak grows again, 2026 cases match 2025 total

News Brief
Ohio’s ongoing measles outbreak has reached 45 cases across 10 counties as of August 2026, matching the state’s entire 2025 total and signaling a third wave this year. Health officials attribute the resurgence to vaccination rates below the 95% herd-immunity threshold, with statewide MMR coverage at 91.5% in 2023. Nationally, U.S. measles cases have hit a 35-year high with 2,295 reported in 2026, surpassing last year’s total. Public health warnings emphasize the preventable nature of the disease and the heightened risk for unvaccinated children and immunocompromised individuals, who face complications including pneumonia and encephalitis. Ohio’s Department of Health and local agencies are conducting contact tracing and issuing exposure alerts while urging renewed vaccination efforts ahead of the back-to-school season.

Tuscarawas County added new cases to its recent outbreak, and Wayne and Trumbull counties are reporting new cases, per the Ohio Department of Health. Ohio reported 45 measles cases in all of 2025. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, surpassing the total from the previous year. Health officials attribute the rise in cases to declining vaccination rates, which have fallen below the 95% threshold for herd immunity. Ohio's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues to grow, spreading to several new counties and equaling the total number of cases from 2025 at the last count. Wayne and Trumbull counties are now reporting cases, their health departments recently announced. And the Ohio Department of Health has added cases in Holmes and Knox counties as of Aug. 6. Tuscarawas has confirmed additional cases as of Aug. 5, bringing its total to eight. Statewide, the ODH is reporting 45 cases in 10 counties, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Aug. 6. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Nationwide, measles cases nationwide hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. Data released July 22 by the Johns Hopkins University shows 2,295 measles cases have been reported nationwide so far this year, surpassing the 2,286 infections recorded during all of 2025 and marking the highest annual total since 1991. Measles growing in Ohio; counties reporting cases Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashtabula: 7 Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 7 Geauga: 13 Holmes: 1 Knox: 1 Ottawa: 1 Trumbull: 2 Tuscarawas: 8 Wayne: 2 The latest case count reflects two previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with 45 in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Where are measles cases spreading? The Wayne County Health Department reports both its cases are likely associated with a previously identified measles exposure event at Smokey Lane Stables, 686 Smokey Lane Road NW in Sugarcreek, on July 17. One of the Tuscarawas cases is from the same event, that health department reports. Geauga Public Health reports that the individuals with measles visited several locations while able to spread the virus, including Bloomfield Horse Sale, 2211 Kinsman Road NW, on July 18. The Tuscarawas health department reports that another of its cases was exposed at the Bloomfield Livestock Auction, at the same address as Bloomfield Horse Sale, on on the same day. In Trumbull County, health officials reports that infected individuals were at the Walmart in Liberty on July 29 and Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department in Boardman on Aug. 2, during the time they were able to spread the virus. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Health experts say the recent surge has been fueled by declining vaccination rates that have left more communities vulnerable to outbreaks. Why are measles cases on the rise? Ohio vaccination rates Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventives, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine ahead of back-to-school season. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023, the most recent year available from ODH. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but it can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. Featured Weekly Ad

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Health expert offers safety tips as measles cases continue to rise in Pennsylvania - wjactv.com

News Brief
Health experts in Pennsylvania are raising concerns about the rising number of measles cases, urging the public to take precautions to prevent the spread of the virus. According to the Pennsylvania Department of Health, there have been 200+ reported infections statewide, with a particular focus on Centre and Jefferson counties. The measles virus is highly transmissible, often spreading before symptoms appear, which makes containment challenging. Matt Ferrari, director of the Center for Infectious Disease Dynamics at Penn State, highlights that the virus is more severe in children who may not have received both doses of the MMR vaccine. Experts warn of potential neurological complications and emphasize the importance of vaccination to protect both individuals and the community. The situation remains under investigation, with ongoing efforts to monitor and manage the outbreak.

WATCHREAD MORE TRUMP NEWS Health expert offers safety tips as measles cases continue to rise in Pennsylvania by Anna Licastro Thu, August 6, 2026 at 5:14 PM Updated Thu, August 6, 2026 at 5:16 PM Health expert discusses rise in measles cases 0Comment Share topics: Measles Pennsylvania MMR Vaccination Public health Penn State Department of Health Vaccine dashboard CENTRE COUNTY, Pa (WJAC) — As measles cases become a growing concern across the state, experts are encouraging the public to take precautions to help stop the spread. While case numbers are still low, experts are emphasizing that measles can be very severe, which is why you should take steps to protect yourself and your community. RELATED | DOH: Measles cases reported in Centre, Jefferson as statewide infections total 200+ “One of the concerning things about measles is that it can be transmissible for 4 to 5 days before you even start showing symptoms. That’s one of the reasons it transmits so quickly is because people don’t even know they’re sick yet,” explained Matt Ferrari, director for the Center for Infectious Disease Dynamics at Penn State. Ferrari says that the measles virus is typically seen more in children, partially due to the fact that many young kids have not had both doses of the MMR vaccine. Powered By 10 519 0 Health expert offers safety tips as measles cases continue to rise in Pennsylvania Share Next Stay “The disease is more severe in children, causes severe fever which can also lead to swelling and inflammation of the brain which can cause neurological complications.” In addition to fever and other common illness symptoms, the virus often presents with a severe uncomfortable rash. Ferrari also says that having measles can make you more susceptible to secondary infections later on, including pneumonia. While the virus is highly contagious, experts emphasize that is also very preventable. Ferrari says that getting vaccinated against measles is by far the most effective way to protect yourself and those around you. “Your vaccination protects not only you, but it protects the others in your community. Again, keep in mind that there are children who could be susceptible to measles but are too young to get the vaccine. There are members of our community that are immuno-suppressed and can’t get vaccinated and they’re still at risk of measles. Our choice to get vaccinated protects us as well as protecting all of those folks.” The Pennsylvania Department of Health created a dashboard on its website to track measles cases, as well as vaccinations. In the past three months, the DOH says its staff have given more than 17-hundred vaccine shots. 孟加拉国没有 疫苗 抗性 麻疹 菌株的遗传证据:icddr,b

No genetic evidence of vaccine-resistant measles strain in Bangladesh: icddr,b
新闻简报孟加拉国国际腹泻病研究中心 (icddr,b) 于 2026 年 8 月 2 日宣布,对正在进行的 麻疹病毒 样本进行的基因测序显示,没有 疫苗 逃逸的证据,缓解了人们对病毒可能发生突变从而降低 疫苗 有效性的担忧。初步结果基于 B3 基因型的 55 个完整 基因组 序列,鉴定了 H 蛋白中先前报道的 4 个 突变,这些序列并不表明对 疫苗 诱导的免疫具有抵抗力。自 3 月中旬以来,暴发 是该国多年来最严重的一次,已导致 130,083 人患病,844 人死亡,其中 9 个月以下的婴儿(对于 疫苗接种 来说太小)受影响尤为严重。虽然 76% 完全接种疫苗的住院儿童检测结果呈阳性,但研究人员警告说,这反映了基于医院的抽样,而不是人群层面的 疫苗 失败。尽管报告报道率很高,但仍敦促当局调查免疫差距和错过 疫苗接种 的原因。
查看新闻五要素
时间
Publication date: 02 August 2026; Event period: 15 March 2026 to 02 August 2026.
地点与对象
孟加拉国;受影响人群包括九个月以下的婴儿、符合 疫苗 资格但未接种疫苗的儿童以及住院儿科病例。
事件
icddr,b released preliminary findings on 02 August 2026 confirming no genetic evidence that the circulating measles virus in Bangladesh has evolved to reduce vaccine protection, addressing concerns during the country's ongoing measles outbreak.
规模、影响与风险
Cumulative totals as of 02 August 2026: 130,083 suspected measles cases (16,295 laboratory-confirmed), 844 deaths (748 suspected and 96 laboratory-confirmed measles-related), and 112,620 hospitalizations with 108,283 recoveries. Infants aged three to eight months accounted for 137 confirmed cases, and 97% of infants under nine months tested positive. Among fully vaccinated children, 76% of those hospitalized tested positive, though this does not reflect population-level vaccine effectiveness.
应对、状态与不确定性
icddr,b 及其合作伙伴对 55 个完整的 基因组 序列(B3 基因型)进行了系统发育分析,在之前报道的 H 蛋白中识别出 4 个 突变,但并不表明 疫苗 耐药。研究人员强调,尽管报告覆盖率很高,但仍需要进行基于人群的研究来衡量 疫苗 的有效性并调查免疫差距。敦促当局确定儿童错过 疫苗接种 的原因并解决潜在的社区层面的免疫差距。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

No genetic evidence of vaccine-resistant measles strain in Bangladesh: icddr,b

News Brief
Bangladesh’s International Centre for Diarrhoeal Disease Research (icddr,b) announced on 2 August 2026 that genetic sequencing of measles virus samples from the ongoing outbreak shows no evidence of vaccine escape, easing concerns that the virus may have mutated to reduce vaccine effectiveness. The preliminary findings, based on 55 whole-genome sequences of the B3 genotype, identified four previously reported mutations in the H protein that do not indicate resistance to vaccine-induced immunity. The outbreak, the country’s worst in years, has sickened 130,083 people and killed 844 since mid-March, with infants under nine months—too young for vaccination—disproportionately affected. While 76% of fully vaccinated hospitalized children tested positive, researchers caution this reflects hospital-based sampling and not population-level vaccine failure. Authorities are urged to investigate immunity gaps and reasons for missed vaccinations despite high reported coverage.
Original source text (English)

TBS Report 02 August, 2026, 06:45 pm Last modified: 03 August, 2026, 09:29 am Infographic: TBS An investigation by the International Centre for Diarrhoeal Disease Research, Bangladesh (icddr,b) has found no genetic evidence that the measles virus circulating in Bangladesh has evolved to reduce the protection offered by the existing vaccine, easing concerns amid the country's worst measles outbreak in years. The preliminary findings, released today (2 August), also identified infants younger than nine months -- too young to receive the first routine measles-rubella (MR) vaccine dose -- as the hardest-hit age group. The findings come as the outbreak continues to spread. According to the Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS), four more children died from measles and measles-like symptoms in the 24 hours until 8am today, raising the combined death toll since 15 March to 844, including 748 suspected and 96 laboratory-confirmed measles-related deaths. During the same reporting period, 1,022 new suspected and 115 laboratory-confirmed cases were recorded, taking the cumulative totals to 130,083 suspected and 16,295 confirmed infections. Since the outbreak began, 112,620 suspected patients have been hospitalised, of whom 108,283 have recovered. Keep updated, follow The Business Standard's Google news channel Infants most affected To investigate concerns that the virus had mutated, researchers from icddr,b and Bangladesh Shishu Hospital and Institute (BSH&I) studied 341 children aged up to 14 years admitted with suspected measles between 20 April and 21 May. Laboratory tests confirmed infection in 324 children. Children aged three to eight months accounted for the largest affected group, with 137 confirmed cases. Researchers grouped the children by age and vaccination status. Among 146 infants below nine months, 142 (97%) tested positive. Among 126 vaccine-eligible but unvaccinated children, 122 (97%) were positive. Of 48 children who had received one vaccine dose, 44 (92%) tested positive, while 16 of 21 (76%) who had received both recommended doses were confirmed with measles. Researchers stressed this does not mean 76% of all fully vaccinated children contract measles, as the study involved only hospitalised children already admitted with suspected infection. The lower positivity among vaccinated children is consistent with vaccine protection, although a population-based study is needed to measure vaccine effectiveness during the outbreak. "The size of this outbreak has understandably raised concerns about whether the vaccine is still working. Our findings provide no evidence that the virus has escaped vaccine protection," said Dr Noorjahan Maliha, associate scientist at icddr,b's Virology Laboratory. She said the hospital-based investigation cannot provide a population-level estimate of vaccine effectiveness, while the 16 infections among fully vaccinated children require further investigation. No evidence of vaccine escape Researchers conducted phylogenetic analysis of 55 whole-genome sequences – 18 sequenced by icddr,b and 37 by the Institute of Epidemiology, Disease Control and Research (IEDCR). All belonged to the B3 genotype, which has also caused outbreaks in several other countries. Although four mutations were identified in the virus's H protein, researchers said they have been reported previously and do not indicate resistance to vaccine-induced immunity. Window of vulnerability The findings also highlight a vulnerable window for infants. In Bangladesh, the first routine MR vaccine is given at nine months and the second at 15 months. Before receiving the first dose, infants rely partly on maternal antibodies and community protection created through widespread vaccination. An analysis of stored samples from 16 mother-child pairs found maternal measles antibodies declined rapidly, with no protective antibodies detected among children aged three to eight months. "Vaccination remains the most effective way to prevent measles and reduce the risk of severe illness," said Dr Mustafizur Rahman, senior director of icddr,b's Infectious Diseases Division. He called for investigations into why such a large outbreak occurred despite high reported vaccination coverage. Dr Kamrun Nahar, assistant scientist at the Virology Laboratory, said authorities also need to determine why children miss vaccination and whether national coverage masks communities with significant immunity gaps. 没有证据表明 疫苗 被 麻疹病毒 逃脱:icddr,b 研究

No evidence of vaccine escape by measles virus: icddr,b study
新闻简报icddr,b 与孟加拉国 Shishu 医院和研究所合作进行的一项研究发现,没有任何遗传证据表明目前在孟加拉国流行的 麻疹病毒 已逃脱现有 麻疹-风疹 疫苗 的保护。该调查分析了2026年4月20日至5月21日期间341名疑似麻疹住院儿童的样本,确认了324例麻疹病例。在 9 个月以下的婴儿中(对于 疫苗接种 来说太小),146 名儿童中有 142 名检测呈阳性,而 126 名未接种疫苗的儿童中有 122 名以及 48 名仅接种一剂的儿童中有 44 名检测呈阳性。相比之下,接受两种推荐剂量的 21 名儿童中有 16 名检测结果呈阳性,表明完全接种疫苗的儿童中阳性率较低。遗传分析显示该病毒属于B3基因型,尽管在H蛋白抗体识别区域检测到了四个疫苗,但没有证据表明疫苗逃逸。研究人员警告说,这些基于医院的研究结果无法估计人群水平的 疫苗 有效性,并呼吁进行更大规模的研究,以了解为什么尽管全国覆盖率很高,但 疫苗 仍会传播。icddr,b 启动了扩大的 监测、队列研究 研究和社区研究,以完善免疫策略并解决免疫差距。

时间
发布于 2026-08-02;活动期间2026年4月20日至5月21日。

孟加拉国;研究人群:孟加拉国 Shishu 医院和研究所收治的 341 名 14 岁以下疑似 麻疹 儿童。
事件
icddr,b 与孟加拉国 Shishu 医院和研究所合作进行了一项实验室确认的调查,得出的结论是没有遗传证据表明循环中的 麻疹病毒 已经逃脱了 疫苗 诱导的免疫。

341例疑似病例中,324例为实验室确诊的麻疹;146 名 <9 个月婴儿(尚未符合 疫苗接种 资格)中有 142 名检测呈阳性;126名未接种疫苗的儿童中有122人检测结果呈阳性;48 名接受过一剂疫苗的儿童中有 44 人检测结果呈阳性;接种两剂疫苗的 21 名儿童中有 16 名检测结果呈阳性。循环病毒为B3基因型,与其他国家的毒株密切相关,没有疫苗逃逸的证据;检测到四个 H 蛋白 突变,但与降低的 疫苗 保护无关。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

No evidence of vaccine escape by measles virus: icddr,b study

News Brief
A study by icddr,b, in partnership with Bangladesh Shishu Hospital and Institute, has found no genetic evidence that the measles virus currently circulating in Bangladesh has escaped protection from the existing measles-rubella vaccine. The investigation, which analyzed samples from 341 hospitalized children with suspected measles between 20 April and 21 May 2026, confirmed measles in 324 cases. Among infants under nine months—too young for vaccination—142 of 146 tested positive, while 122 of 126 unvaccinated children and 44 of 48 children who received only one dose tested positive. In contrast, 16 of 21 children who had received both recommended doses tested positive, indicating lower positivity among fully vaccinated children. Genetic analysis showed the virus belongs to genotype B3, with no evidence of vaccine escape despite the detection of four mutations in antibody-recognized regions of the H protein. Researchers caution that these hospital-based findings cannot estimate population-level vaccine effectiveness and call for larger studies to understand why the outbreak spread despite high national coverage. icddr,b has launched expanded surveillance, cohort studies, and community research to refine immunization strategies and address immunity gaps.
Original source text (English)
YouTube No evidence of vaccine escape by measles virus: icddr,b study Latest Bangladesh Business World Sports Opinion Politics Diplomacy More E-Paper ☀️ 🌐 English বাংলা Russian Hindi Chinese Trending US-Iran Talks Gold Price Measles Outbreak Trump Iran War × Today's Newspaper Latest World Cup Magazine 2026 Election 2026 Follow Us Print: 07 Aug 2026 Health No evidence of vaccine escape by measles virus: icddr,b study Daily Sun Report, Dhaka Published: 02 Aug 2026, 08:41 PM Updated: 03 Aug 2026, 01:57 AM Share Share options Print Copy link Facebook X WhatsApp Linkedin A A Preliminary findings from an icddr,b investigation have found no genetic evidence that the measles virus circulating in Bangladesh has escaped the protection provided by the existing vaccine. According to a press release issued by icddr,b on Sunday, the investigation, conducted jointly with Bangladesh Shishu Hospital and Institute (BSH&I), involved 341 children aged up to 14 years who were admitted to Bangladesh Shishu Hospital & Institute with suspected measles between 20 April and 21 May. Laboratory testing confirmed measles infection in 324 of them. The findings showed that children who had received both recommended doses of the measles-rubella vaccine were less likely to test positive for measles than those who were unvaccinated or had received only one dose. Among 146 infants below nine months of age, who were not yet eligible for routine vaccination, 142 tested positive for measles. Of 126 children old enough to be vaccinated but who had received no dose, 122 tested positive. Among 48 children who had received one dose, 44 tested positive, while 16 of the 21 children who had received both recommended doses tested positive. The researchers cautioned that these figures should not be interpreted as showing that 76% of fully vaccinated children contract measles, as the investigation included only children who had already been admitted to hospital with suspected measles. They said the progressively lower positivity rate among fully vaccinated children was consistent with vaccination providing protection, although a larger population-based study would be needed to determine vaccine effectiveness during the outbreak. Genetic analysis found that the circulating virus belongs to the B3 genotype, which has been associated with outbreaks in several other countries. icddr,b conducted phylogenetic analysis of 55 whole-genome virus sequences, including 18 sequenced by icddr,b and 37 by the Institute of Epidemiology, Disease Control and Research (IEDCR). The analysis showed that all viruses belonged to the B3 subclade and were closely related to strains reported elsewhere, with no evidence that the virus had evolved to evade vaccine-induced immunity. Researchers identified four mutations in regions of the virus's H protein recognised by antibodies. However, they said similar mutations had previously been reported in measles viruses circulating in other countries and that their presence does not indicate resistance to the vaccine. Further laboratory studies are needed to determine whether the mutations have any measurable effect on antibody protection. The investigation also highlighted a period of vulnerability among young infants before they receive the first routine measles-rubella vaccine at nine months of age. An initial analysis of stored samples from 16 mother-child pairs from an earlier icddr,b birth cohort found that maternal measles antibodies declined rapidly during the first months of life. However, researchers said the sample size was too small to draw definitive conclusions and that further investigation was required. "The size of this outbreak has understandably raised concerns about whether the vaccine is still working. Our findings provide no evidence that the virus has escaped vaccine protection. We observed lower positivity among children who had received both doses, although this hospital-based investigation cannot provide a population-level estimate of vaccine effectiveness," said Dr Noorjahan Maliha, associate scientist at the Virology Laboratory of the Infectious Diseases Division at icddr,b. Dr Mustafizur Rahman, senior director of the Infectious Diseases Division at icddr,b, said vaccination remains the most effective way to prevent measles and reduce the risk of severe illness. He said further research is needed to determine why the outbreak became so widespread despite comparatively high reported national vaccination coverage and whether immunity gaps exist in some communities. Dr KamrunNahar, assistant scientist at the Virology Laboratory, said studies would also examine why some children miss vaccination and help refine Bangladesh's immunisation strategy. icddr,b Executive Director Dr Tahmeed Ahmed said the organisation had initiated a broader research programme to investigate why some vaccinated children became infected, factors contributing to severe disease and death, the role of malnutrition, and the decline of maternal antibodies in infants. In response to the outbreak, icddr,b has launched a clinical cohort study across four divisional hospitals, expanded laboratory and genomic surveillance, an enlarged birth-cohort immunology study, a pilot study on zinc alongside standard measles treatment, and community research into why children miss vaccination. The researchers urged parents and caregivers not to delay routine measles-rubella vaccination and to ensure that children receive all recommended doses in accordance with the government's immunisation programme. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

韦恩县、特朗布尔县各报告 2 例与俄亥俄州 麻疹 相关的确诊 麻疹 病例 - 雅虎

Wayne, Trumbull counties each report 2 confirmed measles cases tied to Ohio outbreak - Yahoo
新闻简报俄亥俄州东北部另外两个县(韦恩县和特朗布尔县)于 8 月 5 日各报告了两例确诊的 麻疹 病例,扩大了全州范围内的 麻疹 范围,目前包括阿什塔比拉县、塔斯卡拉瓦斯县和格奥加县。在韦恩县,这些病例与 7 月 17 日在 Sugarcreek 的 Smokey Lane Stables 发生的暴露事件有关,其中一例疑似病例正在接受调查。特朗伯尔县于 7 月 29 日在一家沃尔玛商店和阿克伦儿童马哈宁谷急诊部发现了暴露情况,促使卫生官员警告未接种疫苗的个人在 21 天内监测症状。俄亥俄州卫生部和当地卫生区正在进行接触者追踪,安排 疫苗接种 诊所,并敦促居民寻求 疫苗接种 并在出现症状时避免与公众接触。麻疹 具有高度传染性,传播 可能在出疹前四天至出疹后四天内发生。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-05;据报道,活动日期为 7 月 17 日、7 月 29 日和 8 月 5 日(发布日期)。
地点与对象
美国俄亥俄州东北部韦恩县和特朗伯尔县;斯莫基巷马厩(韦恩县)和沃尔玛(特朗伯尔县)以及阿克伦儿童马哈宁谷急诊部(特朗伯尔县)的受影响个体。
事件
俄亥俄州东北部的两个县报告了确诊的 麻疹 病例,作为全州 麻疹 的一部分;韦恩县官员于 7 月 17 日宣布了两例与斯莫基巷马厩暴露事件有关的确诊病例,特朗布尔县报告了两例与沃尔玛和阿克伦儿童马哈宁谷急诊室暴露事件有关的确诊病例。
规模、影响与风险
韦恩县和特朗布尔县有四例确诊的 麻疹 病例;韦恩县正在调查一例疑似病例;全州范围内的 暴发 正在处理阿什塔比拉县、图斯卡拉瓦斯县和格奥加县的先前病例;麻疹具有高度传染性,从出疹前四天到出疹后四天传播;确定了暴露地点并发布了公共卫生警告。
应对、状态与不确定性
韦恩县和特朗布尔县的卫生部门正在调查接触情况、监测接触者并进行接触者追踪;疫苗接种 诊所安排在特朗伯尔县;建议公众在接触后 21 天监测症状,如果出现症状,请联系医疗保健提供者,如果被感染,请避免前往公共场所;疫苗接种强调最好的保护。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wayne, Trumbull counties each report 2 confirmed measles cases tied to Ohio outbreak - Yahoo

News Brief
Two additional counties in Northeast Ohio—Wayne and Trumbull—each reported two confirmed measles cases on August 5, expanding a statewide outbreak that now includes Ashtabula, Tuscarawas, and Geauga counties. In Wayne County, the cases are linked to a July 17 exposure at Smokey Lane Stables in Sugarcreek, with one suspected case under investigation. Trumbull County identified exposures at a Walmart store on July 29 and Akron Children’s Mahoning Valley Emergency Department, prompting health officials to warn unvaccinated individuals to monitor symptoms for 21 days. The Ohio Department of Health and local health districts are conducting contact tracing, scheduling vaccination clinics, and urging residents to seek vaccination and avoid public contact if symptomatic. Measles is highly contagious, with transmission possible from four days before to four days after rash onset.
Original source text (English)
Wayne, Trumbull counties each report 2 confirmed measles cases tied to Ohio outbreak - Two more counties in Northeast Ohio, Trumbull and Wayne, have reported confirmed cases of measles as the statewide outbreak continues. Two more counties in Northeast Ohio are reporting measles cases as the statewide outbreak continues. On Wednesday, health officials in Trumbull and Wayne counties each announced two confirmed cases of measles. Last week, the Ohio Department of Health announced a measles outbreak in the state amid cases in Ashtabula and Tuscarawas counties. On Tuesday, health officials in Geauga County announced five measles cases. WAYNE COUNTY According to the Wayne County Health Department, both of their confirmed cases are "likely associated" with a previously announced measles exposure at Smokey Lane Stables in Sugarcreek on July 17. "The individuals were exposed during the event and later developed symptoms consistent with measles," the department stated in a release. "Other people from other communities were also exposed and contracted measles at this event." Wayne County health officials add that an additional "suspected case" is being investigated as of Wednesday afternoon. "We are actively monitoring Wayne County residents who may have been exposed. We are asking residents to be aware of symptoms and to take precautions when seeking treatment," advised Wayne County Health Commissioner Nicholas Cascarelli. "Without a doubt, being vaccinated against the measles virus still offers the best protection. It is safe and it works." TRUMBULL COUNTY The Trumbull County Combined Health District said it is working with the Ohio Department of Health and Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department to investigate potential exposures and identify anyone who may have been exposed. According to the health district, one of the individuals visited the following locations while contagious: - Walmart, 200 Goldie Road in Liberty Township, on July 29. - Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department, 6505 Market St. in Boardman, on Aug. 2. Health officials said anyone who visited those locations and is not vaccinated against measles, is unsure of their vaccination status or has developed symptoms after possible exposure should contact a healthcare provider. The health district recommends that unvaccinated individuals monitor themselves for signs and symptoms of measles for 21 days after any possible exposure. Last week, the Ohio Department of Health announced a measles outbreak in the state amid cases in Ashtabula and Tuscarawas counties. On Tuesday, health officials in Geauga County announced five measles cases. Symptoms of measles include a high fever, cough, runny nose, watery eyes and a rash that typically develops three to five days after other symptoms begin. Officials said measles is highly contagious and can remain in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves an area. People can spread the virus from four days before a rash appears until four days after it develops. The Trumbull County Combined Health District also announced two upcoming measles vaccination clinics: - Thursday, Aug. 6: Mesopotamia Township Hall, 8800 state Route 534, from 9-11 a.m. and 1-3 p.m. - Tuesday, Aug. 11: Trumbull County Combined Health District, 194 W. Main St. in Cortland, from 9-11 a.m. and 1-3 p.m. Residents who believe they have measles are urged to stay home, avoid contact with others and call their healthcare provider before visiting a medical office or hospital so appropriate precautions can be taken
View five news elements
Time
Published on 2026-08-05; event dates reported as July 17, July 29, and August 5 (publication date).
Location and population
Wayne and Trumbull counties, Northeast Ohio, USA; affected individuals exposed at Smokey Lane Stables (Wayne County) and Walmart (Trumbull County) and Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department (Trumbull County).
Event
Two counties in Northeast Ohio reported confirmed measles cases as part of a statewide outbreak; Wayne County officials announced two confirmed cases linked to an exposure event at Smokey Lane Stables on July 17, and Trumbull County reported two confirmed cases with exposures at Walmart and Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department.
Scale, impact and risk
Four confirmed measles cases across Wayne and Trumbull counties; one suspected case under investigation in Wayne County; statewide outbreak ongoing with prior cases in Ashtabula, Tuscarawas, and Geauga counties; measles is highly contagious, spreading from four days before rash onset to four days after; exposure locations identified and public health warnings issued.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health departments in Wayne and Trumbull counties are investigating exposures, monitoring contacts, and conducting contact tracing; vaccination clinics scheduled in Trumbull County; public advised to monitor symptoms for 21 days post-exposure, contact healthcare providers if symptomatic, and avoid public settings if infected; vaccination emphasized as best protection.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

俄亥俄州最新的 麻疹 暴发 新增病例翻倍 - Akron Beacon Journal

Ohio's latest measles outbreak doubles with new cases - Akron Beacon Journal
新闻简报俄亥俄州卫生部门于 8 月 5 日证实,在来自 Geauga 县和 Tuscarawas 县的 7 份新报告后,俄亥俄州 2026 年第三起 麻疹 暴发 病例增加了一倍多,达到 11 例。其中 6 例病例发生在未接种疫苗的个体中,接触与北布卢姆菲尔德的一次马匹销售和 Sugarcreek 的马厩活动有关。在全国范围内,美国 麻疹 病例已达到 35 年来的最高水平,2026 年迄今报告感染人数为 2,295 例,原因是 麻疹 发病率下降,削弱了群体免疫力。当地卫生部门正在进行接触者追踪并通知接触者进行隔离,而州官员则强调两剂 MMR 疫苗接种 的有效性为 97%。在此 麻疹 期间,俄亥俄州尚未报告与 麻疹 相关的死亡事件。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-05;活动期间2026年7月17日至8月5日。
地点与对象
美国俄亥俄州;受影响的县:阿什塔比拉县、图斯卡拉瓦斯县、格奥加县;与布卢姆菲尔德马匹拍卖会(北布卢姆菲尔德)和斯莫基巷马厩(Sugarcreek)相关的风险;年龄范围 15 个月至 30 岁。
事件
俄亥俄州 2026 年第三次 麻疹 麻疹 正在增长,病例数量增加了一倍多,达到 11 例;由 Geauga 公共卫生和 Tuscarawas 县卫生部门报告;经实验室检测证实。
规模、影响与风险
确诊病例11例;没有死亡报告;畜牧场所的暴露;11 例病例中有 6 例发生在未接种疫苗的个体中;美国全国 麻疹 病例数创 35 年来新高(2026 年 1 月至 7 月 2,295 例);严重并发症的风险,包括肺炎和脑炎。
应对、状态与不确定性
当地卫生部门正在进行接触者追踪和暴露通知;俄亥俄州卫生部 (ODH) 和 Geauga 公共卫生部门推广的 疫苗接种;ODH 敦促 MMR 疫苗接种;俄亥俄州幼儿园 疫苗接种 覆盖率 91.5%(2023 年);未达到群体免疫阈值(>95%);状态:正在进行调查,有待确认。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio's latest measles outbreak doubles with new cases - Akron Beacon Journal

News Brief
Ohio’s third measles outbreak in 2026 has more than doubled to 11 cases after seven new reports from Geauga and Tuscarawas counties, the state health department confirmed on August 5. Six cases occurred in unvaccinated individuals, and exposures were linked to a horse sale in North Bloomfield and a stables event in Sugarcreek. Nationally, U.S. measles cases have reached a 35-year high with 2,295 infections reported so far in 2026, driven by declining vaccination rates that have eroded herd immunity. Local health departments are conducting contact tracing and notifying exposed persons to quarantine, while state officials emphasize the 97% effectiveness of two-dose MMR vaccination. No measles-related deaths have been reported in Ohio during this outbreak.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Entertainment Lifestyle Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals NEWS Ohio's latest measles outbreak doubles with new cases Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Aug. 5, 2026Updated Aug. 6, 2026, 6:50 a.m. ET Ohio is experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with the total number of cases rising to 11 with seven new cases reported. Some infected individuals may have visited a horse sale in North Bloomfield while contagious. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, a trend experts attribute to declining vaccination rates. The best way to protect against measles is through the MMR vaccine, which is 97% effective after two doses. Ohio's third measles outbreak of 2026 is growing, with cases more than doubling amid new reports from two counties. Geauga County is reporting five new cases related to the ongoing measles outbreak, defined as three or more related cases, Geauga Public Health announced on Aug. 4. And the Tuscarawas County Health Department reports another two cases as of Aug. 4, and is awaiting laboratory confirmation on more. Previously, the Ohio Department of Health reported four infected individuals — three in Ashtabula County and one in Tuscarawas County — ranging in age from 4 to 30. This brings the total number of cases in the current outbreak to 11. The six cases in Ashtabula and Tuscarawas were among unvaccinated individuals, ODH and the county health department report. The Geauga health department did not list the vaccination status of its five cases. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Nationwide, measles cases nationwide hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. Data released July 22 by the Johns Hopkins University shows 2,295 measles cases have been reported nationwide so far this year, surpassing the 2,286 infections recorded during all of 2025 and marking the highest annual total since 1991, USA TODAY reports. Geauga County measles outbreak may have spread at a horse sale Geauga Public Health reports that the individuals with measles visited several locations while able to spread the virus, including Bloomfield Horse Sale, 2211 Kinsman Road NW, on July 18. The health department is working with the individuals and their healthcare providers to identify others who may have been exposed, who will be notified by their local health department and provided guidance on quarantining. ODH is working with Geauga Public Health to follow up on potential exposures and promote opportunities for vaccination. The Tuscarawas health department reports that one if its new cases was exposed to measles at the Bloomfield Livestock Auction, at the same address as Bloomfield Horse Sale, on July 18. The other was exposed during a previously identified measles exposure event at Smokey Lane Stables, 686 Smokey Lane Road NW in Sugarcreek, on July 17. Tuscarawas County cases range in age from 15 months to 19 years. Measles cases in Ohio growing According to the ODH Summary of Infectious Diseases dashboard's last update on July 31, the state was reporting 15 total measles cases and two previous outbreaks – three cases in Cuyahoga County in January and seven cases in Franklin County in February. That total does not include the Geauga cases or any others that may have been reported. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with 45 in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Health experts say the recent surge has been fueled by declining vaccination rates that have left more communities vulnerable to outbreaks. Why are measles cases on the rise? Ohio vaccination rates Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventives, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine ahead of back-to-school season. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023, the most recent year available from ODH. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. Measles case confirmed in Holmes County, Ohio - spectrumnews1.com

News Brief
Ohio health authorities confirmed a single laboratory-confirmed measles case in a Holmes County resident on August 5, 2026. The Holmes County General Health District is actively tracing potential exposure sites and urging vaccination as part of broader statewide efforts to curb rising measles activity. This year, Ohio has recorded 15 confirmed cases, already exceeding the 2025 total of 45, while national figures show 2,318 cases in 2026—more than the entire previous year. Measles, spread through respiratory droplets and highly contagious even before symptoms appear, poses an elevated risk in communities with suboptimal MMR vaccination coverage, which in Ohio remain below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity.
Original source text (English)
OHIO — The Holmes County General Health District confirmed Tuesday that it was notified of a laboratory-confirmed case of measles in a resident. Currently, the health district is working to identify potential exposure sites and encourage vaccination. Anyone who is identified as potentially exposed will be contacted by public health officials and given guidance on next steps. Last week, the Ohio Department of Health announced the state’s third measles outbreak of this year. So far in 2026, there have been 15 confirmed measles cases. The state had 45 cases in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Nationally, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention is reporting 2,318 cases so far this year, already surpassing the number of infections for the entire year of 2025. Measles, which is extremely contagious, is spread through sneezing and coughing. The virus can live up to two hours in the air where the infected person coughed or sneezed. Once breathed in, an individual can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. Symptoms include: - Rash - High fever - Running nose - Cough - Loss of appetite - Red, watery eyes The CDC recommends all children to get two doses of the MMR vaccine — the first dose at 12 through 15 months of age, and the second dose at 4 through 6 years of age. Adults who are unvaccinated or don't know if they received the vaccine can also get one. CDC data shows that vaccination rates for the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine in Ohio are below the benchmark of the recommended 95% by health officials, as they say 95% vaccination rates in a community can help develop herd immunity to a disease

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles surging across the country; does NJ have any cases? - northjersey.com

News Brief
A nationwide measles surge in the United States has reached nearly 2,400 confirmed cases in 2026, according to CDC data, marking a sharp rise from 285 cases in 2024 and 59 in 2023. The outbreaks span 37 states, with Utah, South Carolina, Virginia, Florida, and Texas accounting for roughly 1,900 cases. Measles, an airborne virus with up to two-hour surface viability, poses the highest risk to children under five, pregnant women, and adults over 30. In New Jersey, only one confirmed case has been reported this year. Public health authorities emphasize vaccination as the primary preventive measure, alongside hand hygiene and mask use, while the World Health Organization advises vitamin A supplementation for infected children to reduce complications.
Original source text (English)
Son of ex-Tenafly councilman sentenced in child porn case | Exclusive News Bergen Sports HS Sports Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals NEWS Measles surging across the country; does NJ have any cases? Juan Carlos Castillo USA TODAY NETWORK Aug. 4, 2026Updated Aug. 5, 2026, 7:30 a.m. ET There have been nearly 2,400 confirmed cases of measles in the U.S. this year, the vast majority of them associated with one of the 37 measles outbreaks reported this year, according to the latest numbers from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). In comparison, there were only 285 cases in 2024 and 59 in 2023. U.S. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. is now saying that parents should vaccinate their children against measles. Here's what to know about the disease in New Jersey: Are there measles cases in New Jersey? Utah, South Carolina, Virginia, Florida and Texas have the highest number of confirmed cases this year, accounting for roughly 1,900 cases out of the close to 2,400 reported this year. Utah leads with 524 cases. New Jersey is among the states with the fewest cases. According to the CDC, there has been only one confirmed case in the state so far this year. Can adults get measles? Any person that is not immune to the virus can get measles regardless of age. Measles infection tends to be more severe in children under five, pregnant women and adults over 30. What is measles and how does it spread? Measles is an airborne viral illness considered one of the world’s most contagious diseases. It can lead to severe complications and death, particularly in children under 5 and pregnant women. As an airborne virus, it spreads through tiny nasal and throat droplets in the air and on surfaces after those who are infected sneeze or cough. Once the virus is in the air or on surfaces, it can remain active for up to two hours. What can parents do to minimize risk of measles? Vaccination is the most effective way to prevent measles. The vaccine comes in two doses that are usually administered before age 2. If your child is infected with measles, keeping them well-nourished and providing vitamin A supplements can reduce the risk of complications, according to the World Health Organization. Washing hands often and using N95 facemasks can reduce the risk of contracting measles, according to the Occupational Safety and Health Administration. The measles vaccine is considered safe and affordable and often is combined with immunization for mumps and rubella.
